Table of Contents

\*\*CAMPBELL / KNOTT GENEALOGICAL PROJECT\*\*

\*Settlement Geography Framework | V30 base \+ V31–V74 updates | August 2026\*

\*Evidence codes: \[A\] \= directly in source | \[B\] \= inference from source | \[C\] \= project hypothesis requiring primary source confirmation\*

\# \*\*1. PURPOSE AND SCOPE OF THIS DOCUMENT\*\*

V67 integrates: Glenlyon Campbell III (2021) — the project’s standing secondary-synthesis source — re-examined across two passages: the Florence Wesley/Lillian Ellwood second-marriage narrative, and the 107th Battalion military section (formation through Campbell’s death). Source ingestion for the military section was conducted externally (Rook/GPT) and cross-checked against the V66 primary record per standing protocol; factual claims taken as verified, contradictions tested internally. \*\*Lillian Ellwood identified \[B — family narrative, uncited\]:\*\* the book identifies her as Glen’s personal nurse, with whom he began a relationship before going overseas; she accompanied him to England and remained there for the war’s duration. This gives the V66 Ellwood bequest (BoM Waterloo Place, London; “now at H.O. London”) a coherent narrative context for the first time, though the identification carries no cited source (the passage’s only footnote, fn 36, is an internal cross-reference to the Timber Wolves narrative at p.40, not a source citation) and is not promoted above \[B\]. Florence is identified as Glen’s \*\*secretary during his time with the Indian Agency\*\* \[B\] — new occupational datum, consistent with his 1912 Chief Inspector role and the DCB’s “young Englishwoman living in Winnipeg.” \*\*Standing source-reliability note established for this work’s second-marriage chronology:\*\* the book states the marriage occurred “three years after Harriet’s death” (c.1913) and “ended in 1915” — both directly contradicted by the V66 primary record, which shows Florence as wife-beneficiary on pay cards raised at Glen’s 3 January 1916 enlistment, continuously styled “wife” through the 1917 death processing and the 1918 Affidavit of Survivors, and in receipt of a widow’s pension from 1 January 1918 — i.e., the legal marriage demonstrably subsisted to Glen’s death. The book’s dates are not adopted; a possible reconciliation (informal separation c.1915–16 coinciding with the Ellwood relationship, consistent with the will’s structure) is logged at \[C\] only. This is treated analogously to the existing Bryce reliability note (Section 5): Glenlyon Campbell III (2021) remains a high-quality, prosopographically strong secondary synthesis and is not devalued generally, but its specific claims are weighed against primary sources where both exist, with this chronology as the documented instance.

\*\*107th Battalion military section \[A/B mixed, coded below\]:\*\* Contemporary primary sources newly identified via the book’s citations: \*\*Manitoba Free Press, 18 Aug 1917\*\* — a Campbell letter after Vimy quoting him directly (“We came here as a unit, so the 107th will always live”), reporting Vimy casualties (10 killed, 25 wounded) and naming five men transferred to the 16th Battalion whom Campbell continued to track (Tiny Leonard, Scott Parcher, James Randle, Sandy Braid, Colin Dow; only Leonard and Braid wounded at time of writing) \[A\]. \*\*Manitoba Free Press, 14 Aug 1918\*\* — a former officer quotes a cemetery sergeant: “Colonel Campbell must have been a very popular man. I’ve had more inquiries for his grave than any in the cemetery” \[A\] — independent contemporary evidence of Campbell’s reputation among serving soldiers. A \*\*2 October 1917 letter to a Dauphin newspaper\*\* reports Campbell tired of the war, in worsening health, and longing to return home \[A\] — eighteen days before death, cohering tightly with the V66-documented cause of death (calculus ureter and toxaemia); the book’s narrative of chronic kidney disease and sepsis is now independently and materially strengthened by the V66 primary medical record. \*\*Recruitment/formation:\*\* confirms Campbell’s late-1914 Indigenous Battalion proposal was rejected, and he subsequently raised the 79th and then the 107th Battalions \[A, no conflict with framework\] — this converges with the existing Thomas Johnstone entry (Section 6.4.2/V52), whose CEF record shows enlistment in the 79th Battalion (July 1915\) prior to transfer into the 107th: if Campbell raised both units, Johnstone’s full enlistment path ran through Campbell-raised battalions end to end, logged as circumstantial network evidence \[B\] bearing on, but not resolving, the paternity question. \*\*114th Battalion (Brock’s Rangers) transfers\*\* brought Indigenous composition to roughly 500+ soldiers, with A Company reportedly entirely Indigenous except NCOs \[A\]. \*\*Family service confirmed together:\*\* Jack Campbell, Thomas Johnstone, and “Jack” (John William) Waddy served alongside Campbell \[A\] — the Waddy service connection independently corroborates the V66 will-witness finding and supplies his family-usage forename. \*\*Maison Blanche graffiti\*\* (battalion insignia, Indigenous names and tribal affiliations, including a Timber Wolf cap-badge carving attributed to Walter McNicol, later killed the first day of Vimy) is physical evidence connecting identifiable soldiers to the Vimy sector \[A soldier identifications; interpretation of spatial association with 16th Battalion transfers held at \[B\]\]. \*\*Vimy:\*\* Campbell reportedly refused to begin forward work until respirators arrived \[B pending War Diary confirmation\]; first 107th casualties from a shell at an ammunition dump \[A\]; transferred 107th men buried at C40 — Fred Bagulay, Alfred Kirby, Francis Manning, Robert Pattison, Henry McWilliams, Joseph Willerton \[A\]. \*\*Hill 70:\*\* continued Pioneer work (roads, tramways, communications, trenches) preceding combat; a volunteer rescue recovered 25 wounded and 30 dead, with Oliver Barron and Andrew Anderson receiving the Military Medal \[A\]; over 80 members gassed in a mustard-gas attack \[A\]; casualties 21 killed, 128 wounded, 93 gassed, 2 missing (Canadian Corps \~8,700) \[A\]. Commendations quoted from Lt.-Col. D.M. Osmond (“assistance and bravery”) and General Currie (“gallantry and devotion to duty”), cited to the War Diary — exact wording flagged for direct verification \[C\]. \*\*Death:\*\* confirmed 20 Oct 1917 at Étaples — matching the V66 probate affidavit exactly and reinforcing the Étaples reading over DCB’s Camiers; Major Hugh Walkem assumed command 23 Oct 1917, also noted as Campbell’s 54th birthday, consistent with his CEF attestation (b. 23 Oct 1863\) \[A, internal coherence check passes\]; posthumous DSO, consistent with framework. \*\*Indigenous participation\*\* is documented prosopographically throughout (named individuals from Kahnawake, Six Nations, Montreal Lake Cree, Lac La Ronge Cree, and Riding Mountain/Keeseekoowenin connections) rather than by aggregate figures — this emphasis is preserved as a framework note rather than itemized individually. \*\*\[B\] interpretive material\*\* (Campbell’s internal thoughts, fear before Passchendaele, the evacuation farewell scene, “beloved Colonel” framing) is coded as narrative interpretation, not evidence. \*\*\[C\] items flagged for direct verification, primarily against the 107th Battalion War Diary (LAC):\*\* exact War Diary wording on the respirator episode; Currie and Osmond commendation texts; origin of the “Timber Wolves” name; choice of regimental march “The Campbells are Coming”; source of the ambulance farewell scene; source of the quoted line “This is one of the happiest days of my life.” The 107th Battalion War Diary is added as a new Tier 2 target, positioned to resolve most of the \[C\] list in a single source. N2, N4, N6, and N8 are not affected this session.

V66 integrates: Glen Campbell’s complete Surrogate Court probate file (Eastern Judicial District; 16 images, FamilySearch/PAM, Image Group 005335575, images 2532–2547) and three pages of his CEF service file (LAC RG150, b1434-s026 series), constituting the largest single primary-source accession on Glen’s death, estate, and second marriage to date. \*\*The will \[A — two independent copies\]:\*\* a soldier’s Form of Will executed 12 February 1917 in the 107th Battalion, held in both a handwritten copy (probate exhibit, form P.85) and a typed copy (service file, form M.F.W. 82); the typed copy settles a handwritten-date ambiguity (1916/1917) in favour of 12 Feb 1917\. Witnesses: Lieut. Hubert J. McCusker and \*\*J.W. Waddy, soldier, 107th Bn\*\* — Christina’s husband, full name confirmed \*\*John William Waddy\*\* on the renunciation, served in Glen’s own battalion and witnessed his will \[A\]; this adds a service-connection datum to the existing Waddy/Wady open question (Section 6.4/14.3) without resolving it. Real estate devised to the four children of the first marriage (John Robert Campbell, then 107th Bn CEF; Elleonora Knott, Gilbert Plains; Christina Waddy, 17 Gordon Apts, Victor St, Winnipeg; Mary Cameron Campbell, Gordon Apts, Winnipeg — apartment number differs between copies, 14 vs. 19, unresolved transcription variance); personal estate divided equally among the same four except Bank of Montreal Winnipeg monies to Mary Cameron Campbell alone and Bank of Montreal, Waterloo Place, London monies bequeathed to \*\*Lillian Ellwood, lately resident at 107 Wentworth Block, Winnipeg (Portage Ave), now at\*\*\*\* \*\*\*\*“\*\*\*\*H.O. London\*\*\*\*”\*\* \[A — both copies; “Home Office” expansion held at \[C\], ambiguous between the British government department and a firm’s London home office\] — a new named individual, identity and relationship unknown, logged without interpretation; Henderson’s Winnipeg Directory (1915–17) identified as the primary probe. \*\*Florence and Hugh appear nowhere in either copy of the will\*\* \[A\] — the exclusion is structural in the instrument itself, refining the DCB account (which remains the sole source for the children-persuaded-him explanation). \*\*Estate administration \[A\]:\*\* no executor named; the four children, “all of Gilbert Plains,” jointly renounced probate 17 July 1918 in favour of the Union Trust Company Limited (witness Bruce Brown, clerk, Gilbert Plains; sworn before Duncan Cameron, Commissioner — the flour-mill partner’s continued civic role); letters of administration with will annexed granted 12 December 1918; estate duty $232.85; solicitors Tupper, McTavish, Foley & Tupper (partner roster includes a Glenlyon C. McDonald, commissioner on most affidavits — forename logged without interpretation); estates manager Alfred L. Bartelmeh. The Tupper letter (26 Nov 1918\) responding to Judge Myers’s requisition attests “of our own knowledge” that Glen \*\*was married before making the will — in fact before going overseas\*\* \[A — solicitor attestation\], bracketing the Florence Wesley marriage before Glen’s embarkation and before the Feb 1917 will. \*\*Death record refined \[A\]:\*\* Militia & Defence certificate (27 Nov 1918): died No. 20 General Hospital, France, 20 Oct 1917, \*\*of calculus ureter and toxaemia\*\* — first documented cause of death, explaining the struck “Died of Wounds” entries on the pay cards; the probate affidavit of death gives Étaples, DCB gives Camiers — No. 20 General Hospital lay in the Étaples/Camiers hospital cluster and both readings are carried with the hospital identification as the reconciling fact \[B\]. \*\*The Affidavit of Survivors (13 Nov 1918\) \[A\]\*\* lists John Robert Campbell (son), Elleonora Knott (daughter), Christina Waddy (daughter), Mary Cameron Campbell (daughter), \*\*Florence Campbell (wife), and\*\*\*\* \*\*\*\*“\*\*\*\*H. H. Campbell\*\*\*\*”\*\*\*\* \*\*\*\*(son)\*\* — the first primary-source record of Hugh in the project (Section 6.4.1 gap closed to existence-and-survival-in-1918; the second initial “H.” is new and unexplained). \*\*Inventory and valuation (13 Nov 1918), $29,453.25 \[A\]:\*\* real estate — ⅓ interest in W½ S1 T24 R22 WPM ($4,000); SE¼ S2 T24 R22 ($4,500, mortgage $1,295); NW¼ and Fr. SW¼ S19 T56 R26 WPM ($800/$150 — a northern Manitoba holding not previously in the framework, new open item); E½ S1 T24 R22 sold to J.J. Drinkwater for $4,000 under agreement for sale dated 30 June 1916 at 8% ($3,051.60 owing); second mortgage held on N½ S8 T24 R23 (mortgagor George Rutledge, $2,110 from Dec 1913); livestock 17 horses/37 cattle/49 sheep/2 goats/6 pigs; 486 bus. barley, 350 lbs wool; cash Sterling Bank Gilbert Plains $3,556.54 and Bank of Montreal Winnipeg $2,297.60; military pay balance $25.75. \*\*Harriet devolution reading \[B\]:\*\* with Harriet predeceasing Glen (17 May 1910; Section 6.5 — the Settlers of the Plains account of her death at the Riding Mountain Ranch is now, per the V65 Merchiston/Riding Mountain Ranch identity finding, the same statement as family memory that she “died at Merchiston”), Glen’s ⅓ interest in W½ S1 is consistent with a devolved share of her scrip parcel (NW¼ \+ N½SW¼ S1 falls within W½ S1), with the remaining shares plausibly with the children — completing the chain Harriet (1895 patent) → partial devolution incl. Glen’s ⅓ (1910–17) → J.R. Campbell on the 1923 Cummins map; held at \[B\] (\[C\] for the ⅓ arithmetic) pending a Land Titles abstract for S1 T24 R22, which is added as a new Tier 2 target capable of resolving patent → estate → sale in one document set. Two land opens: the E½ S1 Drinkwater parcel lies outside Harriet’s patent and Glen’s acquisition of it is undocumented; Glen’s own S½ S12 T24 R22 patent parcel (1901) is absent from the estate entirely — disposed before death or omitted, open. A minor internal variance is harmonized: Section 6.4’s “died … at Elphinstone” and Section 6.5’s died-at-the-ranch account are read as registration-district vs. physical location. \*\*CEF service file pages \[A — LAC RG150\]:\*\* Glen’s enlistment date confirmed as \*\*3 January 1916\*\* (Separation & Assigned Pay Branch ledger); Florence documented as wife-beneficiary across three cards with a four-point address sequence — \*\*210 Maryland St. → 619 Sherbrooke St. → 204 Good St., Winnipeg → c/o Imperial Bank, Victoria, B.C.\*\* — establishing her as a documented Winnipeg resident through the war and her subsequent relocation to Victoria, B.C. in the pension period; separation allowance $60/mo, assignment $75 effective 1 Nov 1917, account suspended 31 Jan 1918, \*\*widow’s pension granted effective 1 January 1918\*\* (processed 13 Dec 1917), overpayment recovery through 1919\. The statutory allowance/pension track ran independently of, and untouched by, the will’s exclusion — stated explicitly to forestall any apparent contradiction. On this evidence the V61 Saskatchewan 1921-census Florence candidate (RM of Weldon, domestic servant, “single”) is \*\*closed as ruled out\*\* — wrong province, wrong trajectory; her post-1918 search anchor is now Victoria, B.C. (directories; 1921 census B.C.). The Wesley marriage window is bracketed documentarily (wife-beneficiary status on a 3 Jan 1916 enlistment \+ the Tupper pre-embarkation attestation), consistent with DCB’s 1916 but still \[B\] pending a marriage registration. New names logged without interpretation: Lillian Ellwood; Alfred L. Bartelmeh; Bruce Brown; Glenlyon C. McDonald; J.H. Goldsmith (pensions clerk); Mrs. M. Blakely, Chatham, N.B. (cheque-mailing error only). The RG150 access route used here is flagged for a fresh pass on the outstanding Alfred William Knott military-record inquiry (Section 14.1). N2, N4, N6, and N8 are not affected this session.

V65 integrates: a three-source convergence on the Merchiston Ranch founding sequence and the Riding Mountain House post history, culminating in primary Crown patent instruments. (1) Rev. Dr. Bryce’s career biography of Robert Campbell (“A Great Scottish Fur Trader,” 11 Dec 1901, copied from Fraser’s Scottish Annual 1901\) corroborates the Merchiston name and Riding Mountain location and supplies an independent 1880 land-take-up statement, Kildonan burial, the “Glenlyon House” naming precedent (Simpson’s Tower, 1837), and annual/semi-annual Winnipeg trips from Merchiston \[A — for Bryce’s direct recollections; he met Campbell in 1871 and knew him at Kildonan\]. Bryce is demonstrably unreliable on dates/places outside direct experience — his 1863 marriage date is impossible (children born Fort Chipewyan 1860/1861 against the documented 5 Aug 1859 Norway House marriage \[A — E.48/7\]) and his “Edinburgh” death-place for Elleonora conflicts with the documented Comrie record — a standing source-reliability note is logged: Bryce alone promotes nothing to \[A\]. (2) The Four Arrows (La Cuatro Fletchas A.C.) “Riding Mountain House” research compilation (6 pp., footnoted to HBCA, Fort Ellice journals, Traill journal, Selwood 1983\) supplies a continuous post chronology c.1850–1895 integrated at Section 5.8/6.1: Fort Ellice outpost at Lake Audy c.1850 (named for James Audy), 1854 winter post, named clerk succession 1862–79, the 1867 burning of the post by the band over provisioning grievances, Robert Campbell’s direct 1868 crisis involvement at Fort Ellice (with Archibald McDonald) \[A — Traill journal\], Walter Traill’s 1868 rebuild “to where the Merchiston Ranch was to be located” (explicit post-site/ranch-site identity), Okanase (“Little Bone Chief”) tariff renegotiation, the 15–16 Sep 1875 Treaty annuity payment at the post to the “Riding Mountain and Dauphin Lake Band, Skyman Keegeegowemin, Chief” (early chief-title attestation), the 1878 six-mile downriver relocation to s.8, the Jan 1880 Brydges establishment of the Elphinstone townsite (named for Lord Elphinstone, 1879 district guest — origin of the toponym, with the footnoted caveat that Charles Geekie rather than Robert Campbell may have been the host), townsite commercial failure (8 lots/$160; whole townsite later sold for $80 against $275/yr taxes), and the May 1895 post closure with buyer and price (schoolteacher Lauder, $1,941.71). Structurally most significant: the HBC never held title to the original Riding Mountain House land — the post was omitted from the 1869 surrender schedule — and the site (containing Okanase homes and Okanase’s grave) could have been included in the reserve when laid off; logged as directly relevant context for the land-selection sections. Four Arrows fn 30 independently restates the Campbell–Scott origin: Glen returned from 1885 Rebellion service (Bolton’s Scouts) bringing Walter Scott, age 21, friendship formed in the Scouts; Glen married Harriet, Walter married Victoria, Harriet’s younger sister — a second secondary source for the existing \[B\] 1885-origin finding, which remains \[B\] pending primary NWR service evidence for Scott Sr. Four Arrows fn 31 corroborates the existing Section 5.16 Kennis/Atkinson kinship entries. (3) Sixteen Dominion Lands patent images were grid-checked and attributed. Confirmed core instruments \[A\]: Robert Campbell’s patent for SE¼ S34 T18 R21 W1 — the Riding Mountain House site itself — (grantee “of the City of Winnipeg, Gentleman”), resolving the long-standing Section 6.3 R20/R21 discrepancy in favour of R21 and corroborating the 27 Aug 1887 Neepawa filing (Doc 55619); Robert’s Westbourne Settlement river lots 17 & 18 (392 acres, 21 Nov 1883\) and the easterly six chains of lot 27 (19 Jun 1886\) on the White Mud River — holdings at the corridor’s southern staging point; Glenlyon Archibald Campbell’s patent for the fractional E½ S3 T19 R21 W1 (314 acres, 7 Oct 1884\) — family land in the Riding Mountain/Elphinstone block by Oct 1884, predating the 1885 Merchiston advertisement and the 1887 post-site filing and removing any apparent conflict between them; Glen’s patent for S½ S12 T24 R22 W1 (320 acres, 15 Oct 1901, grantee “Farmer and Rancher”), adjoining Harriet’s scrip parcel — a contiguous 560-acre family block at the corridor exit; and Harriet Campbell’s own patent instrument for NW¼ \+ N½SW¼ S1 T24 R22 W1, 240 acres, 17 Jun 1895, grantee styled “of Elphinstone … Wife of Glenlyon Archibald Campbell” — the patent for the scrip land already held at \[A\], now anchored to the instrument itself (marginal N.W.H.B. grant notation flagged for scrip-mechanism cross-check). Four Minnedosa-corridor patents (T14–15 R15–16 W1, incl. two quarters of S10 T15 R16 crossed by the reserved Winnipeg–Fort Ellice highway) carry a resident-farmer styling inconsistent with the confirmed instruments and are held unattributed; four patents (Whitby ON merchant; Alberta 1902; Assiniboia NWT; Holland MB 1903\) are excluded as different Robert Campbells. Net effect: the Merchiston founding sequence is revised and largely settled — family presence in the 18/19-21 block by Oct 1884 \[A\], named Merchiston operation by 1885 \[A — ad\], Robert’s formalization on the old post quarter 1887 \[A — patent\], with the earlier 1878 purchase \[A — PAM finding aid\] and 1880 buildings \[Ringstrom A\] retained as the acquisition/occupation stages. Section 6.3 table revised accordingly; R20/R21 flag retired. A Baldy Scott local-history piece (“Riding Mountain Ranch 1900–1916”) supplies the Merchiston→Riding Mountain Ranch transition mechanism \[B\]: Glen sold or mortgaged Merchiston to raise Klondike money (consistent with the documented 1897–98 expedition \[A\]); Lord Vivian became owner and renamed it; Walter Scott Sr. managed it, stated to have been “with the ranch since Robert Campbell started it”; operational detail 1900–16 (horse operation, Mr. Day of High River as supplier, 1906 barn, Lake Audy Plain and Kennis’s Meadow wintering grounds, Billy Bone and Joe Mentuck as winter herders) — establishing at \[B\] that Merchiston and Lord Vivian’s Riding Mountain Ranch are the same property, previously carried as separate entries. New open name: “Walter Burns,” described with the Flett boys as “nephews of Mrs Scott” (Victoria Burns Scott) — not in the current Burns children/grandchildren roster. N2, N4, N6, and N8 are not affected this session.

V64 integrates: full-text examination of the 1889 Big Grass travel correspondence (correspondent “Whitewood,” dispatch dated 12 Aug) as printed in the Manitoba Free Press of Tuesday 27 August 1889, p.2 — two days before the Weekly edition date (29 Aug 1889\) previously cited; both citations refer to the same piece, and the daily-edition date is adopted as the primary citation with the weekly noted as a reprint \[A\]. Substantive new content from the full text: the toponym “Gilbert Plains” is in print by August 1889 (“The beautiful country, called the ‘Gilbert Plains’”), the earliest dated press attestation of the name now held — five years after the 1884 Ross cabin trade and seventeen years before town incorporation, tightening the interval between the naming event and public adoption and reinforcing the district-before-town sequence (Section 9.8) \[A\]; Big Grass is described as “the first inhabited place” on the Lake Dauphin–Gilbert Plains route, twelve to sixteen miles southwesterly, reached “by way of the ‘Oil Wells’” — a new, unconnected waypoint place-name, logged without interpretation; and a Lake Dauphin settler roster is recorded (Neil McDonald, the Whitmore farm/Mrs. Wm. Whitmore, John Edwards, Chas. Hay, Hewis, Esplin, Jas. Shand), with the settlement described as two years old (\~1887 origin) \[A\]. A Winnipeg Tribune report (16 Jun 1892, p.4) of a 10 June 1892 political meeting at Gartmore, Lake Dauphin, provides primary-source press corroboration of Glen Campbell’s 1892 Opposition candidacy for Dauphin — already on record via MHS (Section 6.4: unsuccessful 1892 and 1896 attempts against Theodore A. Burrows) — and confirms the Burrows identification directly (Burrows spoke for the government side); campaign issues recorded: the school question, N.P.R. freight rates, and Greenway-government economy \[A — factual skeleton; the Tribune’s partisan characterizations are excluded\]. New names logged without interpretation: Gartmore (meeting site), W. Adam (Adam & Co., chair), Dr. Beauchamp, and H.C. Ross (Campbell-side speakers; the Ross surname coincidence is noted but unresolvable from this source). A Winnipeg Tribune notice (25 Jul 1904, p.10) supplies independent newspaper corroboration of the V55 flour-mill finding and adds the formal corporate name — “The Gilbert Plains Milling Co., Ltd.” — and the financing mechanism: a $3,000 municipal bonus, with the by-law to raise the money voted 30 July 1904; the mill was thus a publicly subsidized venture within the municipality Cameron led, tying the commercial nucleus directly into the civic-formation layer; the notice also fixes “Gilbert Plains village” as the location designation in mid-1904 \[A\]. An 1885 newspaper advertisement (“Pure Bred West Highland Bulls For Sale,” signed R. & Glenlyon A. Campbell, Merchiston, Strathclair P.O.) documents Robert and Glen Campbell as joint named principals in a single Highland-cattle breeding enterprise at Merchiston Ranche, with stock imported from Scottish West Highland herds, reared unhoused, and marketed for beef crossing \[A\] — corroborating and dating Robert’s existing first-importer record (Section 6.2 notes) and establishing that the family Highland operation predates, and is the documented district source consistent with, the purebred Highland herd observed at Big Grass in 1889; the Merchiston→Big Grass stock-transfer link itself is held at \[B\], as the ad does not document physical movement between the properties. The 2006 Winnipeg Sun Gilbert Plains centennial souvenir edition (9 Jul 2006\) is logged as a source-context item: it independently confirms the Memory Lane compilation’s production context (576-page hardcover, community-compiled for the centennial, Sharon Basaraba named among the local contributors alongside Terry and Linda Murray, Jack Yuill, Tara Yuill, Audrey Kotak, and Marge Puchalski — potential local-history contacts logged alongside the museum-binders lead), and Premier Doer’s message contains a documented instance of the Robert/Glen conflation in official commemorative material (“Scottish Hudson Bay trader Glenlyon Campbell”) — flagged as a source error, not adopted \[A — article contents; error flagged\]. No open items (N2, N4, N6, N8) are affected this session.

V63 integrates: a substantially strengthened Campbell–Scott partnership thread. Newspaper sources now document the relationship across four decades: earliest confirmed instance is a Manitoba Weekly Free Press travel correspondence piece (29 Aug 1889\) identifying Glenlyon Campbell and Walter Scott \[Sr.\] as joint cattle ranchers at Big Grass, with Campbell holding a large purebred Highland herd and \~60 horses \[A\] — predating the previously-earliest-documented partnership event (the 1897–98 Klondike expedition) by roughly eight years. A follow-up Free Press notice (2 Oct 1890\) updates the Big Grass livestock inventory (300 sheep, 100 cattle, 100+ horses following a Calgary mare purchase) \[A\] and names R. McGregor of Brandon as a district hay-meadow operator — a new, currently unconnected name, logged without interpretation. The Klondike expedition itself is newly dated and detailed: Free Press Prairie Farmer (16 Dec 1897, reprinted from the Minnedosa Tribune) confirms Glen Campbell organized the expedition for early February 1898 via Edmonton and Peace River, with \*\*Walter Scott Sr. explicitly named as his partner\*\*, securing dogs and arrangements \[A\] — upgrading the Klondike venture from a parallel fact to a documented joint undertaking. The same article adds new detail to Robert Campbell (Glen’s father, already on record via the HBC E.48 papers): he is described as an early, pre-rush visitor to the Klondike gold districts who discovered gold but did not pursue it given the district’s remoteness, and who left a chart of the discovery site that Glen hoped to use \[A\] — confirmed as the same Robert Campbell already cited, not a name collision. The partnership’s origin point is logged at \[B\], pending primary-source confirmation: Glen Campbell and Walter Scott Sr. are understood to have met during North-West Rebellion service in 1885, which would tie the 1889 ranching partnership, the 1897–98 Klondike venture, and Scott Sr.’s subsequent Riding Mountain Ranch management role into a single continuous relationship dating from 1885; a primary source for Scott Sr.’s NWR service (parallel to Glen’s already-documented Boulton’s Scouts record) is added to the Tier 2 target list. This sustained partnership also raises, without resolving, the plausibility of the previously-flagged \[C\] question of whether Walter Scott Sr.’s and Baldy Scott’s later independent California ranching connections reflect the same Campbell-adjacent network. Separately: a Free Press Prairie Farmer retrospective (15 Jul 1914, reporting on events of 17 Nov 1908\) documents a bye-election in Gilbert Plains — understood to be provincial \[B — per project lead\] — triggered by Glen Campbell’s election to the House of Commons; won by Duncan Cameron (894 votes) over A.D. Cummings (855), a Conservative majority of 39 \[A\], adding a precise date and result to Cameron’s already-documented civic career (RM Reeve, 1901/1903–04). A Winnipeg Tribune sports item (20 Jan 1948\) names a William Knott as a member of Vic Wilson’s winning Gilbert Plains curling rink at the Dauphin bonspiel \[A\]; identified at \[B\] with Alfred William Knott on geographic and name-form consistency with his established local usage (“Will Knott”), not independently confirmed by a second identifier — added to the Section 11.6 community-integration table. A Facebook-sourced photograph of the Campbell ranch house (Donna Glen Campbell, two posts, Oct/Dec 2020\) is logged with an unresolved internal date conflict — the same poster captioned the same photo as 1906 in one post and 1903 in a follow-up, with no source given for either date — and is accordingly treated as undated pending better provenance \[flag — open\]. The house is independently confirmed to have stood until it burned down in the 1990s \[A — family account\]. A follow-up post supplies named subjects via a cousin’s copy of the photo: Joe Priest, Joe Priest Sr., John Rowat, John Sutherswaite, Selby “Sib” Smith, Harry Clarkson, Mary Campbell, Mrs. Glen Campbell, and Glenlyon Campbell \[B — family-oral attribution\]; the identity of “Mary Campbell” is explicitly left unresolved, since without a firm photo date it cannot be determined whether this is Mary B. Campbell (b. 1919\) or another family member. A new Tier 2 acquisition candidate is logged: \*Memory Lane: Memories of Gilbert Plains\*\*\*\* \*\*\*\*\*\*&\*\*\*\*\*\* \*\*\*\*District\* (Sharon Basaraba, comp., 2006), located via WorldCat at the University of Alberta, alongside the already-active \*Our People of Riding Mountain House\* acquisition. No open items (N2, N4, N6, N8) are affected this session.

V62 integrates: Baldy Scott (Walter Archibald Scott, 1906–2005) biographical and marriage resolution — Winnipeg Free Press obituary (21 May 2005\) \[A\] corroborates, without revising, his existing framework identity as Harriet Burns Campbell’s nephew by marriage (son of Walter Scott Sr. and Victoria Burns Scott, Victoria being Harriet’s sister and Chief Keeseekoowenin’s daughter). Full marriage sequence for his wife resolved across three independent primary sources: May Lee Jones (b. 27 Jan 1912, Maysville, Oklahoma) married William Taft Paris in Maysville, Oklahoma \[A — Oklahoma marriage register, p.369\]; married Walter A. Scott 11 February 1933, Reno, Nevada \[A — Nevada State Journal, 11 Feb 1933\]. This resolves an apparent Jones/Paris surname discrepancy between the marriage license and the 2005 obituary as sequential marriages (birth name vs. first married name), not a documentary error. A parallel California-ranch connection is newly flagged but not resolved: both Baldy (El Slolyo Ranch, pre-1933 courtship) and his father Walter Scott Sr. (died in California managing/training horses, per existing Section 6 entry) are independently documented in California ranching contexts — logged as an open question \[C\] whether this reflects a shared family/professional network or coincidental parallel migration. A new source acquisition target is opened: \*Our People of Riding Mountain House\* (Walter Archibald Scott, W.A. Scott, Brandon, Man., 2002), located via WorldCat at the University of Alberta, acquisition in progress — elevated from a passive Tier 2 candidate to active acquisition given Baldy’s existing \[B\] oral-history standing in the Flett/Okanase/Keeseekoowenin network (Section 12.2), with potential to upgrade several entries currently sourced to him at second hand. The Manitoba Department of Agriculture and Immigration’s Departmental Reports for 1909, 1910, and 1911 (HathiTrust, uc1.c063205335) are integrated as contextual reinforcement of the existing five-layer immigration infrastructure model: English/Irish/Scotch immigrants are explicitly named as a preferred, capable settler class by provincial immigration commissioners in 1909, two years ahead of Alfred’s arrival \[A\]; 178 Logan Avenue, Winnipeg is confirmed across all three years as the Employment Bureau’s coordination point, consistent with the existing V47.1 citation \[A\]; direct correspondence between J. Bruce Walker (Dominion Immigration, Charing Cross — Section 11.5) and Joseph Burke (Winnipeg Superintendent of Provincial Immigration Employment) is confirmed via Burke’s December 1910 report, evidencing an operational federal–provincial link between two already-documented figures \[A\]; March 1911 is confirmed statistically as the year’s peak month for US-border port-of-entry traffic (2,488 total across Emerson, Gretna, and other frontier ports) — logged explicitly as a parallel, non-Atlantic settler stream distinct from Alfred’s own Liverpool–Halifax route via the SS Tunisian, and not to be conflated with his personal arrival data \[A\]. None of the three reports contain Gilbert Plains, Knott, or Campbell-specific content. A manuscript-track note, non-evidentiary, is logged per existing convention (Section 8.8): Glen Campbell’s initial review of \*The Campbell–Knott Family\* (Edition 1.0) — “quick read… Well done,” with a fuller read promised — contemporaneous with a confirmed July 2026 Gilbert Plains/Riding Mountain/Keeseekoowenin Reserve visit. No open items (N2, N4, N6, N8) are affected this session; no changes to the Tier 1/Tier 2 target list except the Baldy Scott book moving to active acquisition.

V61 integrates: resolution of the Theodora/Theodore Carbert sex-field discrepancy — the long-standing Section 14.1 Tier 1 top-priority item is retired. Direct examination of the 1901 Census of Canada (Manitoba, Marquette, Gilbert Plains, line 7\) confirms Theodore A. Carbert, son of Joseph and Emmarilla Carbert, sex male, b. 4 Feb 1889 — age 22 in 1911, matching the disputed 1911 entry’s age exactly and confirming the “Theodora” reading as an ink-bleed misread of “Theodore,” consistent with the previously-flagged 1916 Edmonton “Theodore A. Carbert” record \[A\]. Audit candidate N9 (Section 9.6 dangling Crowe cross-reference) is resolved: Crowe’s section location (Sec 20, T24 R22W1) is confirmed directly from the 1894 mail petition and added to Section 9.5, removing the previously-dangling “consistent with Section 9.5” claim. A new scholarly source is integrated: the Dictionary of Canadian Biography entry for Archibald Glenlyon Campbell (Desmond Morton, vol. 14) \[A\] — confirming Glen’s full parentage, the 1 April 1886 Harriet Burns marriage and children count, the 1916 Florence Wesley marriage and son Hugh, Florence’s identification as “a young Englishwoman living in Winnipeg,” Glen’s exact death date and place (20 Oct 1917, Camiers, France), and — for the first time from a scholarly rather than family-oral or MHS source — confirmation that Glen’s children from his first marriage persuaded him to exclude Florence from his will. Two candidate records for Florence Wesley/Campbell were examined and are logged as open, not adopted: a Form 30 passenger declaration (Florence May Campbell, husband named Ernest Campbell) is ruled out on husband-name grounds; a 1921 Census of Canada record (Saskatchewan, RM of Weldon, domestic servant, age 30, arrived Canada 1902, marital status “single”) remains an unconfirmed candidate, weighed against the match given the “single” status is difficult to reconcile with Glen’s 1917 death and DCB’s Winnipeg-based description of Florence. A manuscript-track milestone is logged: Stacey Knott’s review of \*The Campbell–Knott Family\* (Edition 1.0) prior to family distribution, representing the first reception of the documented family history as a complete chronological narrative — a significant point of progress within the existing staged-disclosure framing (Section 8.8) — alongside a valid editorial correction she independently identified (implied-complete sibling list) logged for Edition 1.1. Two Tier 2 research targets are added to Section 16/14.2: RG25 (External Affairs, overseas immigration-post administration) and the IRCC Annual Reports (1867–1954), both identified via the CIHS “Guide to Official Canadian Immigration Primary Source Materials” (Robert Shalka), which also independently corroborates the framework’s existing RG17/RG76 distinction. N2, N4, N6, and N8 remain open with no new evidence located this session; N6 is further clarified but not resolved.

Percy Alford) and independently cross-checked against the GRO Civil Registration Marriage Index (Q4 1908, Liskeard registration district, vol. 4a, p.817) — resolving an apparent register-internal date/location conflict as registration-district-vs-parish-of-solemnization, not two competing marriage events; this adds a second, independent Knott–Wormleighton anchor point (alongside Horace James Knott, d. 1981, Wormleighton) to the existing South Warwickshire social-network finding (Section 11.5.15). All audit candidates are treated per the Independent Audit Requirement: diagnostic input requiring independent evidentiary confirmation before integration, not evidence in themselves — see Section 1a.

Carbert” recorded in the 1916 census at Edmonton. \*\*This is NOT resolved at V55\*\* — the V48 examination did not explicitly document the sex column, and the V55 reading is not yet corroborated by independent direct image examination. A new family member is confirmed: Margaret Campbell, daughter of Glenlyon Archibald Campbell and Harriet Burns, born 26 November 1885 (R.M. of Strathclair) and died 1894 — established at \[A\] by convergence of the Manitoba Birth Index, the 1891 Census (age 5, household of Glenlyon A. Campbell), and family tree data. Her absence from the 1917 Dauphin Herald “surviving children” notice and from the MHS four-children summary is explained by predecease rather than contradiction, since both sources are framed around children living at the time of reference (Section 6.4.2). Campbell and Harriet Burns on 24 August 1891 at Strathclair, Manitoba \[A\] — distinct from the 1 April 1886 Keeseekoowenin Reserve date already on record. Margaret’s 1885 birth, Jack’s 1889 birth, and the household’s “Married” status at the June 1891 census all predate this civil registration, which is consistent with — though does not itself document — an earlier reserve-customary union preceding later civil registration; this two-ceremony reading is logged as a \[B\] supported inference, not \[A\] (Section 6.4.1; Section 9.1). A working note, explicitly held outside the evidence structure pending further investigation, records Glen Campbell’s tenure with the Indian Affairs Branch (Chief Inspector of Indian Affairs for the West, 1912\) alongside a hypothesis that Glen may have selectively drawn genteel English immigrant families into the district as marriage prospects for his children (Alfred Knott; the Turner family); the existing chain-migration model (Section 11.5.15) remains the documentary explanation unless and until direct evidence of Glen’s personal selection role is located (Section 14.3). Two open questions are carried forward: whether Christiana Campbell’s husband John W. Waddy is connected to the same Wady/Waddy family as Beatrice Knott’s husband Godfrey “George” Wady of Warmington, Warwickshire; and one item is resolved outright rather than carried forward — the MHS Memorable Manitobans entry gives Nora Campbell’s birth year as 1890, against three independent primary-source confirmations (RG10 record, gravestone, 1891 census) all giving 14 April 1891; the MHS figure is a secondary-source error and Nora’s birthdate stands as documented, with no further action required.

V58 integrates: (1) \*\*1931 Census of Canada\*\* (Manitoba, Dauphin, sub-district 0036, lines 24–29) — Jack Campbell household confirmed at S35 T23 R22W1; three-point spatial continuity established across the 1895 scrip parcel (S1 T24 R22W1), the 1923 Cummins map (S1/S13 cluster, T24 R22W1), and the 1931 census (S35 T23 R22W1). DLS adjacency confirmed: S35 T23 sits immediately south/diagonal of Harriet’s S1 T24 scrip parcel — one section boundary, same immediate neighbourhood across a township line. Previously undocumented son Maurice Campbell (b. \~1927) surfaced; Mary Campbell’s recorded age (13) implies birth \~1917–18, a 1–2 year discrepancy against the established b.1919 — flagged as a minor census-age variance, not a contradiction. (2) \*\*The White Feather — expanded Aunt Mary testimony:\*\* sibling-differentiated suppression responses confirmed — Fred Campbell did not follow the silence and was subjected to racist taunts at school; family vocabulary confirmed: reserve referred to as “Keesee,” reserve relatives as “the Aunties”; Mary forbidden by her father from visiting the reserve; a specific adult avoidance incident documented (curling banquet, deliberate non-recognition of a reserve cousin); closing testimony logged. Glen III’s pre-1968 belief in an Irish family line is logged as a separate, unexplained identity thread — no Irish ancestry located in the tree; possibly a third cover story distinct from the French-ancestry cover already documented. (3) \*\*107th Battalion composition \[A — DCB; CMEA; CBC; APTN\]:\*\* approximately 500 of 860–1,000 men were Indigenous; one of only two purpose-built Indigenous battalions in the CEF (alongside 114th “Brock’s Rangers,” Six Nations). Glen Campbell obtained authorization from Duncan Campbell Scott, Deputy Superintendent General of Indian Affairs, to recruit underage males directly from the Brandon and Elkhorn residential schools (DCB). Suppression paradox interpretation added to Section 7.2 with cross-reference to Section 6.4.3. (4) \*\*Agentic Research Workflow methodology block\*\* added as new Section 1a — formalizes the existing multi-agent pipeline, role definitions, evidence hierarchy, and the maximal-pipeline caveat. Concept: Jon; role refinement and rationale: GPT/Rook (June 2026).

V57 integrates: a passage from \*The White Feather\* describing Tom Knott’s travel route with Glen III and John — through Riding Mountain Park, exiting at its southern boundary directly onto the Keeseekoowenin Reserve, then to the cemetery containing Moses Burns and Harriet Burns Campbell — strengthening (not fully resolving) the existing open burial-location flag (Section 6.5) toward the “Indian Cemetery, Elphinstone” reading and away from a Clear Lake location \[B\]. Tom Knott’s role is substantially upgraded from an unverified observation-tier remark to a multiply-corroborated finding: Glen III’s email confirms Tom was caretaker of the Gilbert Plains Museum and shared a passion for history \[A — correspondence\]; Stacey’s independent recollection and \*The White Feather\*’s narrative detail corroborate the same character from two further angles \[B\] (Section 8). Aunt Mary’s testimony is identified precisely: she is Mary B. Campbell (b. 1919), daughter of Jack Campbell and Dorothy Turner, already present in the framework as “Mary Campbell Lynch” (Section 7.3 interview source) and as half of “the Gilbert and Mary Lynch farm” (Section 8.3) — a three-way identity convergence \[B\]. Her testimony establishes a second-generation repetition of the identity-suppression instruction: Jack Campbell warned his own children never to admit Indigenous ancestry, distinct from (and reinforcing) Glen’s instruction to his generation already on record (Section 7.2) \[B\]. Stacey’s independent recollection that Tom told her she was “1/18th Native” is logged as oral-tradition transmission evidence, not a genealogical fraction requiring verification \[B\] (Section 8.5). A continuation note is added to the Stacey Knott Coordination Note (Section 8.5) reflecting her increasing ease discussing documented family history, consistent with — not a revision of — the existing staged-disclosure framing. Glen’s direct statement that the Turner and Knott families already knew each other in England, prior to either family’s emigration, upgrades the existing geographic-proximity inference in Section 11.5.15 to direct oral testimony \[B\]. Finally, a research hypothesis (Rook) is logged at \[C\]: Tom Knott’s documented role as Gilbert Plains Museum caretaker and family historian establishes a plausible — not confirmed — provenance pathway by which family materials could have entered the museum’s holdings, sharpening the rationale for the existing Tier 1 museum-binders item without establishing that any such transfer occurred (Section 14.1).

The organizing narrative remains:

\*Geography → Wagiiwing Homeland System → Transportation Corridors → Stopping Places → Burns / Keeseekoowenin Land Selection → Campbell Settlement → Community Formation → Railway Consolidation → Knott Continuity\*

\# \*\*1a. AGENTIC RESEARCH WORKFLOW\*\*

\#\# System Principle

The research framework is an agentic workflow composed of specialized agents. Each agent performs a defined role within the research pipeline. No individual agent is considered authoritative. Evidence quality is determined by provenance, independent verification, and human review.

The Human Researcher remains the final decision-maker for all evidence integration and framework updates.

The architecture separates retrieval, analysis, hypothesis generation, evidence integration, and final judgment into independent responsibilities. This improves modularity, reduces epistemic drift, and allows individual components to be replaced without altering the overall methodology. \[Concept: Jon; role refinement and rationale: GPT/Rook, June 2026\]

Agent responsibilities describe operational roles within the workflow. They are not statements about the inherent capabilities of any particular model. As implementations change, the role definitions remain unchanged. \[GPT/Rook\]

\#\# Human Researcher (Project Lead)

\*\*Responsibilities:\*\* \- Define research questions. \- Determine research direction and priorities. \- Evaluate evidence quality. \- Approve or reject proposed findings. \- Authorize updates to the master framework. \- Resolve conflicting evidence.

The Human Researcher is the final authority for all conclusions.

\#\# Research Assistant Agent (GPT / Rook)

\*\*Purpose:\*\* Accelerate information discovery and structured analysis.

\*\*Responsibilities:\*\* \- Analyze retrieved evidence. \- Summarize documents. \- Compare records. \- Identify inconsistencies. \- Separate observations, interpretations, and hypotheses. \- Suggest additional research avenues.

\*\*Restrictions:\*\* \- Does not directly modify the master framework. \- Does not promote hypotheses to established evidence.

\#\# Framework Integration Agent (Claude)

\*\*Purpose:\*\* Maintain the integrity and consistency of the research framework.

\*\*Responsibilities:\*\* \- Integrate approved findings. \- Maintain evidence hierarchy. \- Cross-check new findings against existing framework. \- Flag contradictions before integration. \- Produce versioned framework builds. \- Preserve citation and provenance integrity.

\*\*Restrictions:\*\* \- Only integrates findings approved by the Human Researcher. \- Conflicting evidence must be flagged rather than merged automatically.

\#\# Exploratory Agent (Grok)

\*\*Purpose:\*\* Provide alternative perspectives and assist when conventional approaches stall.

\*\*Responsibilities:\*\* \- Suggest alternative search strategies. \- Challenge assumptions. \- Identify overlooked possibilities. \- Generate alternative hypotheses. \- Assist in overcoming research dead ends.

\*\*Restrictions:\*\* \- Exploratory suggestions are treated as hypotheses until independently verified. \- Does not modify the framework.

\#\# Retrieval Agent (Vector Database)

\*\*Purpose:\*\* Retrieve relevant evidence from the local archive.

\*\*Responsibilities:\*\* \- Perform semantic retrieval. \- Return relevant document chunks. \- Retrieve associated images and metadata. \- Preserve source references.

\*\*Restrictions:\*\* \- Performs retrieval only. \- Does not interpret evidence.

\#\# Semantic Search Agent (Local Embedding Model)

\*\*Purpose:\*\* Convert documents into searchable semantic representations.

\*\*Responsibilities:\*\* \- OCR and image captioning where required. \- Chunk documents. \- Generate embeddings. \- Maintain semantic search index.

\*\*Restrictions:\*\* \- Performs extraction and indexing only. \- Does not reason or interpret historical evidence.

\#\# Workflow

Source Documents ↓ Semantic Search Agent ↓ Vector Database ↓ Research Assistant ↓ Exploratory Agent (when required) ↓ Human Review ↓ Framework Integration Agent ↓ Versioned Framework

This is the maximal pipeline — the full sequence when all stages are engaged. In practice, sources frequently enter the workflow directly at Human Review (e.g. a pasted excerpt or fresh upload going straight to the Framework Integration Agent for analysis), skipping the local retrieval/embedding stages. The diagram describes the available depth of the pipeline, not a required sequence for every source.

\#\# Evidence Hierarchy

\- \*\*\[A\]\*\* Primary evidence and independently verified records.

\- \*\*\[B\]\*\* Interpretations supported by available evidence.

\- \*\*\[C\]\*\* Working hypotheses requiring further verification.

No agent may promote information from \[C\] to \[A\] without independent supporting evidence and Human Researcher approval.

\*Note: this hierarchy is already established and in active use throughout the framework. Restated here for completeness within the role-definition section. At build time, treat the canonical definition at the head of this document as authoritative; this section’s statement is a cross-reference, not a second source of truth.\*

\#\# \*\*1a. Independent Audit Requirement \[NEW — V60\]\*\*

Prior to each Vx0 build, the framework document shall undergo an independent audit by a reasoning model capable of: detecting internal contradictions; identifying propagation failures; reviewing evidence classification; identifying integration gaps; and distinguishing fact, interpretation, and hypothesis.

\*Cadence:\* The audit is performed against the most recently completed framework version (e.g., V59 → V60, V69 → V70). Audit findings are considered during construction of the subsequent Vx0 build.

\*Model selection:\* Implementation-defined; may change over time according to capability, availability, cost, or operational constraints. The specific model used in any given cycle is a build-history detail, not part of this methodology.

\*Status of audit output:\* Audit candidates, not authoritative corrections. Findings are not integrated directly into the framework. They are a diagnostic tool identifying areas requiring further investigation — each candidate must independently satisfy the project’s standard evidentiary process (contradiction-testing against the source record and existing evidence coding) before any change is made to the framework.

\# \*\*2. ORIGINAL FRAMEWORK ASSUMPTIONS AND WHERE THEY WERE WRONG\*\*

\#\# \*\*2.1 What V14 Assumed\*\*

V14 was a settlement history framed around individuals and transactions. Its organizing questions were: Who arrived first? What land did they hold? Who sold what to whom? The Ross cabin was treated as an incidental encounter point. The DLS survey was used as a verification tool against individual narrative claims. Kennis Creek, the Audy Trail, and Indigenous occupation were recorded as context. The founding moment was Campbell arriving and trading a pony for Ross’s cabin.

\*\*▶ REFRAME: The founding moment was not Campbell arriving at Ross’s cabin. The founding moment was the Riding Mountain escarpment directing all movement in the region into a small number of usable corridors, one of which ran exactly through the location where Ross had built his cabin.\*\*

\#\# \*\*2.2 What V18–19 Added — The Corridor Model\*\*

V18 introduced the Transportation Corridor Model. Three consecutive DLS township surveys documented trail continuity from the Riding Mountain escarpment (T23 R21) northwest through T24 R21 and into T24 R22 at Section 1\. Settlement nodes aligned with this corridor in sequence. V19 consolidated this into a named explanatory framework.

\*\*▶ REFRAME: The Transportation Corridor Model was correct as a local finding but underframed geographically. It described a specific trail rather than the geographic control that made that trail inevitable.\*\*

\#\# \*\*2.3 The Escarpment Finding — What It Actually Changes\*\*

The 1897 Manitoba provincial map analysis confirmed that the Riding Mountain escarpment operated as a regional-scale geographic control. Multiple distinct transportation routes — the Strathclair Road, the Bird Trail, the Dauphin–Russell Road — all respond to the escarpment’s terrain constraints rather than the Dominion Land Survey grid.

| \*\*Earlier Finding\*\* | \*\*What It Actually Means Under the Escarpment Frame\*\* |  
| \--- | \--- |  
| Trail recorded on DLS surveys entering Big Grass district at Section 1 | The escarpment forced all northwest movement through a limited set of crossings; the trail entering at Sec 1 was the practical exit point of one such crossing corridor |  
| Gilbert Ross’s cabin at the trail entry point | Ross occupied the established stopping point at the escarpment exit — the first sheltered position with water access on the prairie side |  
| Keeseekoowenin winter camps at Kennis Creek and Lake Audy | Winter camps positioned on the terrain-constrained corridor below the escarpment — the same corridor used by HBC, settlers, and later highways |  
| HBC Riding Mountain House at Lake Audy / Sec 34 T18 R21 | The post was built where the escarpment corridor met the lake system — the geographic node that controlled movement between the mountain interior and the prairie edge |  
| Harriet Campbell’s scrip at Section 1 T24 R22W1 | Her scrip parcel was not selected at random. It was the prairie-side stopping place at the exit of the principal corridor through the escarpment |  
| Elphinstone as HBC successor community | Elphinstone sits at a terrain-determined node in the same corridor system, not at an administratively arbitrary location |  
| Modern Gilbert Plains formation around the railway | The railway itself followed the prairie edge of the escarpment corridor; modern Gilbert Plains is a late expression of the same geographic forcing |

\#\# \*\*2.4 The Wagiiwing Finding — What It Actually Changes\*\*

The Anishinabe Homeland History source (V44) confirms that Riding Mountain / Wagiiwing functioned as a complete homeland system — not simply a hunting territory, a corridor zone, or a reserve precursor. The source describes Wagiiwing as an interconnected cultural landscape containing seasonal camps, permanent occupation areas, trail networks, hunting territories, ceremonial sites, vision quest locations, burial grounds, initiation areas, medicinal plant locations, kinship routes, trade routes, navigation landmarks, and sacred sites. \[A — Anishinabe Homeland History, Chapters 3 and 5\]

This finding requires a structural revision to the Transportation Corridor Model introduced at V18–19.

| \*\*V18–19 Model\*\* | \*\*V44 Revision\*\* |  
| \--- | \--- |  
| Indigenous people used the corridors | The corridors were one component of a larger organized homeland system |  
| Corridor → HBC → Settlement | Homeland System → Corridors → HBC → Settlement |  
| Trail network \= infrastructure layer | Trail network \= expression of homeland geography, knowledge system, and kinship routes |

\*\*▶ REFRAME: The Transportation Corridor Model was correct as a finding but underframed in explanatory position. The corridor network was not the foundational layer — it was one output of the Wagiiwing homeland system that preceded it. The escarpment remains the primary geographic control; the homeland system is the primary human response to that geography, predating every subsequent layer.\*\*

The corridor model survives intact. Its findings — trail continuity through T23–T24, DLS-recorded routes, Strathclair Road alignment, Bird Trail, Dauphin–Russell Road — are confirmed and unchanged. What changes is their position in the explanatory hierarchy: they now appear as one component of a pre-existing organized homeland system rather than as the foundational human layer in the study area.

Evidence code: \[A\] Homeland system description in Anishinabe Homeland History; \[B\] hierarchical reframing and framework positioning.

\# \*\*3. GEOGRAPHIC CONTROLS\*\*

\#\# \*\*3.1 The Riding Mountain Escarpment — Primary Controlling Feature\*\*

The Riding Mountain escarpment is the dominant geographic feature structuring the entire study area. It rises approximately 400–500 metres above the prairie surface over a short horizontal distance, creating a physical barrier to east-west movement impassable except at specific topographic breaks, valleys, and creek corridors. Every transportation route, every settlement placement, and every land selection in the Gilbert Plains – Elphinstone – Lake Audy corridor is a response to this barrier.

The 1897 Manitoba provincial map (Bulman Bros. & Co., April 1897\) documents this explicitly. Transportation routes uniformly deviate from the DLS grid to navigate terrain constraints. The Strathclair Road, the Bird Trail, and the Dauphin–Russell Road all reflect escarpment topography rather than survey geometry. \[A — 1897 map\]

\#\# \*\*3.2 Secondary Geographic Controls\*\*

\[A\] Lake Audy / Poneeakesakaekun (‘Bird Landing Lake’) — lake system at the base of the escarpment, forming a natural gathering point at the junction of the mountain interior and the prairie edge

\[A\] Kennis Creek (Kinnis Creek) — creek corridor running along or through the escarpment zone; confirmed as the principal stopping place on the trail across the mountain from its earliest documented use

\[A/B\] The Minnedosa / Sas-kaw-je-wun River system — draining from the mountains westward; corridors along watercourses provided practical movement routes in both summer and winter

\[B\] The wetland and prairie transition zone north of the escarpment — shaped agricultural settlement patterns; determined which parcels had timber access, water, and drainage

\#\# \*\*3.3 The Escarpment as Transportation Bottleneck\*\*

The escarpment created a small number of practical crossing corridors through which all movement was concentrated. These corridors pre-existed every documented human use. Indigenous people used them for seasonal movement and hunting. The HBC used them for post access and trade. Métis trappers and farmers used them for travel and stopping places. Settlers used them as routes to unoccupied land. The railway used the prairie margin of the escarpment for its alignment.

\# \*\*4. TRANSPORTATION CORRIDORS\*\*

\#\# \*\*4.1 Pre-Existing Route Network\*\*

The routes documented in the 1897 provincial map and the DLS survey plans were not created by settlers. They were pre-existing travel corridors formalised into colonial infrastructure during the settlement period. The sequence: Indigenous travel route → informal trail → DLS-recorded track → Colonization Road → provincial highway. \[A — multiple sources\]

The pre-settlement cart trail network that preceded DLS-era colonial roads is documented in two sources added at V42. Harry Baker Brehaut, “The Red River Cart and Trails: The Fur Trade,” MHS Transactions, Series 3, Number 28, 1971–72 season, documents the principal cart trail network across the prairies, with specific material on the Pelly Trail (Fort Pelly → Fort Ellice → Fort Garry corridor), the Carlton Trail, and the Fort Ellice node as a convergence point. Fort Pelly was Robert Campbell’s posting; Fort Ellice was the nearest major trail node to the Riding Mountain corridor. The Pelly Trail on the left bank of the Assiniboine passed near Russell and Binscarth to Fort Ellice — the same Russell corridor visible on the 1897 provincial map. \[B — Brehaut, MHS Transactions Series 3 No. 28\]

The Indigenous Peoples Atlas of Canada, “Red River Carts” (indigenouspeoplesatlasofcanada.ca; Royal Canadian Geographical Society / AFN / ITK / MNC joint publication), provides a map titled “Major Cart Trails Prior to the 1880s” showing Fort Ellice as a major trail convergence node, with routes fanning northwest toward the Riding Mountain / Dauphin corridor. The Atlas notes explicitly that Métis families used Red River carts for migration and resource harvesting — directly applicable to the Burns/Ross family movement patterns in the Riding Mountain corridor. \[B — Indigenous Peoples Atlas of Canada\]

Parks Canada Manuscript Report Series 404 (≈300pp) on the Batoche / Carlton Trail corridor is logged as a known resource but has not been loaded. Brehaut draws on the Carlton Trail as his primary interpretive frame; MRS 404 likely provides the institutional documentation underlying that framework. Flag for future access if Section 4 requires deeper trail network sourcing.

\#\# \*\*4.2 The Principal Corridor — Escarpment Exit to Big Grass\*\*

| \*\*Source\*\* | \*\*Evidence for Corridor\*\* |  
| \--- | \--- |  
| T23 R21W1 DLS survey plan | Trail emerging from escarpment through ‘broken country’ and scattered timber, trending northwest \[A\] |  
| T24 R21W1 DLS survey plan | Same trail continuing north, exiting west through Section 6 \[A\] |  
| Early T24 R22W1 ownership map | Route marked ‘To Strathclair’ at the eastern range boundary \[A\] |  
| Baldy Scott | Winter camps of Okanase band along ‘the old Gilbert Plains on Dauphin Trail’ at Kennis Creek and Lake Audy \[A\] |  
| 1883 Strathclair Dauphin Colonization Trail agreement | Formalised 66 yards of the west trail, now Provincial Trunk Highway No. 354 \[A\] |

| \*\*Node\*\* | \*\*DLS Location\*\* | \*\*Function on Corridor\*\* | \*\*Code\*\* |  
| \--- | \--- | \--- | \--- |  
| Ross cabin / Harriet Campbell scrip | Sec 1 T24 R22W1 | Escarpment exit point — first sheltered stopping place at the prairie edge | \[A\] |  
| Walter Scott military homestead | N½ Sec 4 T24 R22W1 | Trail crossroads — N-S and E-W intersection; first postal node before any building | \[A\] |  
| Glen Campbell military homestead | S½ Sec 12 T24 R22W1 | Ranch operation on corridor between founding cabin and commercial nucleus | \[A\] |  
| Cameron store / Glenlyon Post Office | Sec 22 T24 R22W1 | Documented trail junction; district commercial and postal nucleus | \[A\] |  
| Carbert / Knott farm | Sec 21 T24 R22W1 | Adjacent to Cameron section; Knott’s 1911 placement and later farmstead | \[A\] |

\#\# \*\*4.3 The Strathclair Road\*\*

The Strathclair Road is shown on the 1897 provincial map as a made road extending from the Strathclair area through a natural escarpment opening, passing west of Lake Audy, continuing toward the Riding Mountain House / Keeseekoowenin Reserve area, and reaching the prairie edge near Gilbert Plains. The 1883 Colonization Trail agreement formalised this route. \[A — 1897 map; A — 1883 agreement\]

\#\# \*\*4.4 The Bird Trail\*\*

The Bird Trail is shown on the 1897 provincial map as a dotted trail running along the western escarpment margin, following escarpment contours and drainage features rather than section lines. Named explicitly in 1908 government survey route documentation. \[A — 1897 map; A — Ringstrom 1981\]

\#\# \*\*4.5 The Dauphin–Russell Road\*\*

The Dauphin–Russell Road is depicted on the 1897 provincial map as a diagonal transportation corridor extending northeast from the Fort Ellice–Russell area toward Dauphin. It cuts across the DLS grid and follows a practical travel corridor along the prairie edge of the escarpment rather than survey boundaries. \[A — 1897 map\]

\# \*\*5. WAGIIWING — THE INDIGENOUS HOMELAND\*\*

\#\# \*\*5.1 Framework Restatement\*\*

Riding Mountain / Wagiiwing was not simply a hunting territory, a transportation corridor zone, or a reserve precursor. It was a complete homeland system — an interconnected cultural landscape occupied, organized, and maintained by Anishinabe and Cree-Saulteaux peoples for generations before any HBC post, settler, or survey party arrived. \[A — Anishinabe Homeland History, Chapters 3 and 5\]

The oral histories collected in the Anishinabe Homeland History describe Wagiiwing as “a rich cultural ecosystem of travel, place and narrative.” The source confirms that no rigid territorial boundaries separated communities: land use was cooperative, and Wagiiwing was understood as a shared homeland landscape rather than a set of exclusive territorial claims. \[A\]

The Transportation Corridor Model (Section 4\) remains intact as a finding. Its revised explanatory position is: the trail network was one component of this homeland system. The escarpment remains the primary geographic control. The homeland system was the primary human response to that geography. Every subsequent layer — HBC trading, Métis settlement, Campbell ranching, agricultural homesteading, railway, and national park — was imposed on a landscape that was already fully organized. \[B — see Section 2.4\]

\#\# \*\*5.2 Wagiiwing Homeland — Trail and Transportation Infrastructure\*\*

The Anishinabe Homeland History explicitly confirms a highly developed trail system on Wagiiwing before settler arrival. Trails were used for trade, visiting, hunting, and ceremonial travel. \[A\]

The same source confirms that the Birdtail Trail and the Dauphin–Strathclair Colonization Road represent widened versions of earlier Indigenous routes — not colonial creations. \[A\] This is direct primary-source confirmation of the sequence previously inferred from DLS survey records and the 1897 provincial map: \*Indigenous trail → colonial road → provincial highway.\*

Travel on Wagiiwing was primarily terrestrial: foot travel, dogs as pack animals, horses, and wagons. Multiple sources in the document explicitly state that water was not routinely used as the primary travel medium. \[A\] This is consistent with the escarpment terrain — the mountain interior is crossed by trails, not navigable waterways.

The homeland transportation infrastructure included organized maintenance features:

\[A\] Trees were used to construct corduroy crossings through muskeg areas.

\[A\] Birch trees functioned as communication points — travellers left marks on birch bark indicating their presence and direction.

\[A\] Hills and large rocks served as navigation landmarks.

\[A\] Decayed poplar wood was used to mark paths during night travel.

\[B\] These features indicate an organized transportation and communication infrastructure rather than informal or improvised trail use. The level of deliberate maintenance is consistent with permanent and semi-permanent occupation rather than seasonal passage only.

The transportation sequence supported by this source:

\*Indigenous Trail Network → HBC Routes → Colonization Roads → Railway → Modern Roads\* \[B — consistent with DLS survey and 1897 map evidence\]

\#\# \*\*5.3 Lake Audy as Homeland Node\*\*

Lake Audy / Poneeakesakaekun (‘Bird Landing Lake’) appears repeatedly in the Anishinabe Homeland History as one of the most important locations within Wagiiwing. \[A\]

\[A\] Elders described Lake Audy as a favoured living and land-use site. Family members were remembered as having been born there, lived there, and camped there.

\[A\] The area was noted for high moose density, diverse habitat types, elevated shoreline, and good visibility for wildlife and travellers — a combination of subsistence, defensive, and navigational advantages.

\[A\] Oral histories reference cabins on the east side of Lake Audy.

\[A\] Wild rice was planted at Lake Audy before 1933 in an attempt to attract ducks and geese.

Lake Audy / Poneeakesakaekun was already recorded in V43 as a secondary geographic control (Section 3.2) and as a winter camp location for the Okanase band (Section 5.2 / now 5.9). The Anishinabe Homeland History upgrades its status: Lake Audy was not simply a geographic feature or a camp location — it was a named, inhabited, actively maintained homeland node with multi-generational occupation history. \[B\]

\#\# \*\*5.4 Hunting Landscape and Ecological Knowledge\*\*

\[A\] Elder testimony recorded a hunting territory extending from Lake Audy to the eastern escarpment.

\[A\] An elk jump is documented on the east side of the escarpment — a feature requiring detailed landscape knowledge, collective coordination, and long-term occupancy.

\[A\] Salt lick areas were identified and used for predictable hunting success.

\[A\] Moose concentrations were documented around Lake Audy — consistent with the habitat description (diverse habitat types, elevated shoreline).

\[B\] Hunting practice relied on accumulated ecological knowledge rather than opportunistic harvesting. The elk jump, salt lick locations, and moose concentration areas collectively indicate a managed hunting landscape, not casual use.

The Anishinabe Homeland History defines Traditional Technology (TT) as a system of practical ecological and cultural knowledge, explicitly described as a resource in its own right. TT encompassed hunting locations, salt licks, springs, camps, medicinal plants, navigation landmarks, seasonal movements, and resource areas. \[A\] The source records that TT diminished following restrictions on access and reduced opportunities for intergenerational transmission — a direct consequence of the displacement process documented at Section 5.14. \[A\]

\[B\] Knowledge itself must be understood as a homeland resource alongside timber, game, fish, and medicinal plants. The loss of access to Wagiiwing was simultaneously a loss of the conditions under which this knowledge could be transmitted.

\#\# \*\*5.5 Sacred and Ceremonial Landscape\*\*

\[A\] Thunderbird nesting sites existed within Wagiiwing and were considered places to avoid.

\[A\] Vision quest sites were used for multi-day fasting and spiritual practice.

\[A\] Petroglyph locations are recorded in oral history.

\[A\] Multiple burial grounds were identified.

\[A\] Tree burials were documented as a traditional practice.

\[A\] A specific initiation area existed for young women entering adulthood.

\[B\] The homeland geography of Wagiiwing included sacred and ceremonial landscapes alongside economic and subsistence areas. These are not separable layers: medicinal plants, vision quest sites, burial grounds, and hunting territories were all components of the same integrated homeland system.

\#\# \*\*5.6 Traditional Medicines\*\*

The Anishinabe Homeland History records medicinal uses for the following plants: \[A\]

Seneca Root; Sweetgrass; Wild ginger (Wikay); Jack pine; Cat tail; Yarrow-type medicines; Wild sarsaparilla.

\[A\] Seneca Root functioned in both traditional and cash economies — it was both a medicine and a tradeable commodity.

\[A\] Mrs. Mentuck, documented in the Kennis Creek community record (Section 5.16), is confirmed in this source as providing healing and midwifery services around Kinnis Creek and Lake Audy.

\[B\] Medical knowledge formed part of the homeland knowledge system and was transmitted through the same kinship and community networks as ecological and ceremonial knowledge.

\#\# \*\*5.7 Toponymy — Homeland Organized Through Meaningful Places\*\*

The Anishinabe Homeland History records Indigenous place names as a structured system encoding resources, lakes, landmarks, individuals, historical events, and cultural significance. \[A\]

Examples include: Birch Lake; Moose Lake; Salt Lake; Moon Lake; Hanging Man’s Bluff; George’s Spruce Tree; Mooduck’s Dwelling.

\[B\] The homeland geography of Wagiiwing was organized through meaningful places rather than through survey coordinates. The DLS grid — which imposed a uniform geometric system over this landscape — is a late colonial overlay on a pre-existing named and storied geography. The Indigenous toponymy documents the homeland system that the DLS grid displaced without erasing.

\#\# \*\*5.8 HBC / Riding Mountain House / Elphinstone — Successor to the Homeland\*\*

The Anishinabe Homeland History confirms that Cree and Ojibwa peoples frequented Riding Mountain House I in the early 1860s. \[A\] The first post reportedly failed and was abandoned. A replacement post was subsequently established near Elphinstone. \[A\] The source records that Indigenous leadership negotiated improved fur prices prior to the establishment of the replacement post — indicating organized, deliberate engagement with the HBC rather than passive trading. \[A\]

\[B\] Elphinstone represents a successor HBC node serving a pre-existing Indigenous trade landscape. The decision to relocate the post to Elphinstone was not a colonial imposition on an empty terrain: it was a commercial adaptation to where the homeland population was and what trade relationships already existed.

\*\*Timber — The First Large-Scale Commercial Extraction:\*\*

The Anishinabe Homeland History records that timber from the western Riding Mountain cuesta was shipped by the sternwheeler Northwest on 20 May 1881\. The cargo reportedly sold for $22,500 while the vessel itself cost approximately $27,000. \[A\]

\[B\] This single cargo figure demonstrates the extraordinary commercial value of Riding Mountain timber immediately before large-scale agricultural settlement arrived in the Big Grass district. It is the first documented instance of large-scale commercial exploitation of the escarpment resource base — preceding the timber reserve designation (1895), the sawmill operations near Elphinstone, the log drives on the Little Saskatchewan, and the multiple later sawmill establishments. The 1881 Northwest cargo therefore functions as a bridge finding: it marks the transition from the homeland geography era into the commercial extraction era that preceded and enabled settlement.

\*\*\[V65\] Riding Mountain House — continuous post chronology and the title finding (Four Arrows compilation):\*\* A footnoted research compilation (La Cuatro Fletchas A.C./Four Arrows, citing HBCA, Fort Ellice journals, the Traill journal, and Selwood 1983\) supplies a continuous chronology: Fort Ellice outpost on the east side of Lake Audy c.1850, named for HBC employee James Audy; winter post established 1854; Okanase (Michel Cardinal) family seasonally camped just south of the post at SE¼ s.34-18-21; named clerk succession 1862–79 (Berston, Prince, McNab, “Jerry” Ballenden McKay, Postmaster McKay, Traill, Audy, Smith, Armit); post burned by the band in 1867 over poor provisioning and premature abandonment; Robert Campbell (with Archibald McDonald) directly involved in the 1868 response at Fort Ellice \[A — Traill journal\]; Traill’s 1868 rebuild sited south on the Little Saskatchewan “to where the Merchiston Ranch was to be located”; Okanase (“Little Bone Chief”) successfully demanded season-long operation and a tariff revision as conditions of re-establishment — primary documentation of Indigenous negotiating leverage already noted in this section at \[A\]; winter receipts tripled and a permanent post was ordered; Treaty annuity paid at the post 15–16 Sep 1875 to the “Riding Mountain and Dauphin Lake Band, Skyman Keegeegowemin, Chief” \[A — early chief-title attestation; cross-referenced to Section 5.11\]; 1878 relocation six miles downriver to s.8; Elphinstone townsite formally established Jan 1880 by HBC Land Commissioner Brydges at 8-18-21-1, named for Lord Elphinstone, an 1879 district guest (fn caveat: Charles Geekie, not Robert Campbell, may have been the host) — origin of the Elphinstone toponym; townsite commercially failed (8 lots sold, $160; entire townsite later sold for $80 to avoid $275/yr taxes); post closed May 1895 and sold to schoolteacher Lauder for $1,941.71 — adding buyer and price to the existing 1895 closure record. \*\*Title finding \[A — per compilation’s reading of the 1869 surrender schedule\]:\*\* the original Riding Mountain House land was omitted from the HBC surrender document; the Company never received title. The site — containing the homes of Okanase people and Okanase’s own grave — could have been included in the reserve when it was laid off. This is direct context for the land-selection sections: the post site’s exclusion from the reserve was a title accident, not a reflection of the land’s status or use.

\#\# \*\*5.9 The Okanase / Cardinal Family — Long-Term Corridor Occupants\*\*

Michael Cardinal (Okanase, ‘Little Bones’, c.1790–1870) came to the Riding Mountain area around 1800 from the Yellowhead Pass area. His band — identified in reserve records as Cree-Saulteaux — established their primary occupation of the Riding Mountain corridor.

\[A\] Summer camping grounds: SE¼ Section 34-18-21 WPM — immediately adjacent to the HBC Riding Mountain House site

\[A\] Winter camps: west shore Wasagaming, Lake Audy, Kennis Creek — along the corridor documented by DLS surveys and the 1897 map

\[B\] Buffalo hunting: ‘The Hill of the Buffalo Chase’ (Naowawgunwuja) — the mountain name itself records the escarpment’s role in the hunting economy

\[A\] Kennis Creek: ‘popular stopping place in the early days when the trail across the mountain was starting to be used by white settlers’ — confirms the trail pre-existed white settlement

\#\# \*\*5.10 Michael Cardinal’s Family Structure — Corridor Network\*\*

| \*\*Wife\*\* | \*\*Identity\*\* | \*\*Relevant Children / Connections\*\* |  
| \--- | \--- | \--- |  
| Assiniboine/Stoney wife | Named Josephte (Barkwell) | Chief Meekis; Chief Louis Ochoup (O’Soup); Baptiste Cardinal; Chief Red Pheasant — network extending west and south |  
| Orkney wife | Sarah (Sally) — Saulteaux woman; previously recorded as ‘possibly Sarah/Sally Whitford; from Dog Creek area of Lake Manitoba’ | Chief Keeseekoowenin (Moses Burns, b.\~1818/19); Baptese Bone (b.1836); Yellowhead — the Riding Mountain core family. Sarah (Sally) later married James Peter Whitford Sr.; their daughter Margaret (‘Peggy’) Whitford is Michael Cardinal’s half-sister. \[A — Louis O’Soup biographical PDF, Métis Museum\] |  
| Métis (French-Saulteaux) wife | Lizette Slater (b.1831) — scrip records | William Mucatehpenese/Blackbird; George Bone; Antoine Bone — Pine Creek and Camperville corridor |

\*\*\[V43\] Flett / Cardinal / Whitford Kinship Resolution:\*\* The long-standing question of how the Flett family was related to the Okanase/Cardinal/Keeseekoowenin family is now resolved through primary source evidence. The Louis O’Soup biographical PDF (Métis Museum) establishes the following genealogical structure: Jacques Cardinal married Sarah (Sally), a Saulteaux woman; their children included Michael Cardinal (Chief Okanase).
