Sarah (Sally) subsequently married James Peter Whitford Sr.; their daughter was Margaret (‘Peggy’) Whitford, who married George Flett. Michael Cardinal and Margaret Whitford were therefore half-siblings — making George Flett’s children relatives of the Okanase family through this shared maternal line. \[A — Louis O’Soup biographical PDF, Métis Museum\]

This resolves three previously conflicting evidence streams: (1) the HBCA employee biography recording James Peter Whitford’s marriage to an Indigenous woman Sarah, and their daughter Margaret (Peggy) marrying George Flett; (2) the Manitoba History article in which George Flett described Okanase chiefs as relatives (Donaghy, Osborne Lauder, Walter Scott, and George Bryce traditions); and (3) the DCB Keeseekoowenin biography stating that Chief Okanase’s sister Margaret married George Flett — now interpretable as a compressed rendering of the half-sibling relationship rather than a separate marriage requiring a different genealogical path. \[B — reconciliation interpretation\]

The previously open working note ‘James Peter Whitford profile: Cardinal connection remains an unresolved lead’ is retired. \[A\]

\#\# \*\*5.11 Chief Keeseekoowenin (Moses Burns, c.1818/19–1906) — The Corridor’s Central Figure\*\*

Moses Burns, christened Keeseekoowenin (‘Sky Man’) by Rev. George Flett, was the chief of the Riding Mountain band from Mekis’s death in 1874 until his own death in April 1906\. He is the connecting figure between every line of this research. His daughters married the two founding settlers of the Big Grass district. His family’s scrip selections defined the founding land base.

\[V50 addition\] Keeseekoowenin was born in the Bow River region of present-day Alberta; he moved with his family and band to the Riding Mountain region in 1822\. He succeeded his half-brother Mekis as chief upon Mekis’s death in the winter of 1874–75 (the project has previously recorded Mekis’s death year as 1874; the Bow River origin detail refines but does not contradict the existing chronology). The band signed Treaty Two in 1871; as a result, the reserve was relocated to a place near Elphinstone in 1875 — giving a precise sequence for the reserve’s establishment at its terrain-determined location already documented in Section 5\. \[A — Métis Museum biographical entry, Keeseekoowenin (Moses Burns)\]

\[V50 addition\] Rev. George Flett’s mission, established at the original reserve site c.1873, was welcomed in part because Flett’s own cousins were already part of the band and received him warmly — a detail distinct from, and additional to, the existing finding that Flett was Keeseekoowenin’s first cousin (Section 5.12). This indicates a broader pre-existing kinship cluster around the mission’s establishment, not a single cousin relationship. Keeseekoowenin himself held out longest among band members before eventually being baptized as Moses Burns by Flett; the Clear Lake faction of the band remained Catholic and the two groups grew apart over time — a rift later widened by government and church officials who promoted the larger Presbyterian-convert group as exemplary Christian farmers in contrast to the smaller, more traditional hunting and fishing group at Clear Lake. \[B — Métis Museum biographical entry; interpretive framing of the rift attributed to the source\]

\[V50 addition\] By 1906, concerns were already circulating in political circles about closing the Clear Lake Reserve. These concerns are documented as preceding, and arguably setting the administrative groundwork for, the eventual establishment of Riding Mountain National Park in 1929\. This gives an earlier starting point for the displacement process than the 1930/1935 eviction sequence already documented in Section 5; the two should be read as a single continuous process beginning as early as 1906, not two unconnected episodes. \[B — Métis Museum biographical entry, consistent with existing Parks Canada boundary history interpretation\]

\[V50 addition\] Keeseekoowenin became blind shortly before his death at age 88 and was succeeded in the chieftainship by his half-brother George Bone. This adds a final succession data point to the chieftainship sequence already documented (Mekis → Keeseekoowenin); George Bone is already named elsewhere in this framework as part of the Pine Creek/Camperville corridor family cluster (Section 5), and this finding connects that figure directly to the chieftainship succession. \[A — Métis Museum biographical entry\]

\[V50 addition\] Chief Okanase’s Nakota wife bore sons Louis O’Soup, St Paul (possibly Jean-Baptiste Lolo, also known as St Paul), and Mekis (Eagle) — refining the existing polygamous-marriage structure recorded in Section 5 by naming the specific sons of the Nakota wife as distinct from the Métis-wife sons already documented (George, John, William, and Antoine Bone). Mekis (Eagle), as a son of the Nakota wife, is therefore a half-brother to Michael Cardinal (Chief Okanase’s son by a different union) and to Keeseekoowenin himself, consistent with the half-brother succession already noted above. \[B — Métis Museum biographical entry\]

Moses Burns gravestone — V33 addition: The gravestone in the Keeseekoowenin cemetery was photographed in The White Feather (Glen Campbell, 2024). It reads: ‘Moses Burns. Died at Elphinstone Apr 10, 1906\. Aged 87 yrs. Chief of Okanase Indian Reserve at Riding Mountain.’ Age 87 gives birth year \~1819 — consistent with project estimate of c.1818. His formal title as recorded on the stone: Chief of Okanase Indian Reserve at Riding Mountain. \[A — gravestone photograph\]

Robert Campbell called him ‘one of Nature’s gentlemen.’ \[B — Baldy Scott\]

Known children (RM Strathclair history, most complete list): sons George, David, and Solomon; daughters Mrs Cook, Mrs Glen Campbell (Harriet), Mrs Fish, Mrs McLeod, Mrs Walter Scott (Victoria), and Bella. Scott Jr. records three sons and seven daughters in total. \[A — RM Strathclair history; A — Scott Jr.\]

George Flett Burns (eldest son): christened by Rev. George Flett; family subsequently used the Flett surname. 150 acres under cultivation. His sons Alex Flett and Solomon Flett were killed in action in WWI; their names are inscribed on the Cenotaph in Strathclair. \[A — RM Strathclair history\] Solomon Burns: identified as the best farmer on the reserve, with 185 acres under cultivation; a Presbyterian leader. \[A — RM Strathclair history\] David Burns: trapper, hunter, and guide. Accompanied Glen Campbell and Walter Scott on the Klondike expedition 1898\. Subsequently served as Lord Vivian’s hunting guide on the Riding Mountain Ranch. \[A — Settlers of the Plains, 1953; B — Our Story to 1970\]

\#\# \*\*5.12 Rev. George Flett — Not an Outsider Missionary\*\*

Rev. George Flett was Keeseekoowenin’s first cousin \[B — Baldy Scott\]. He was born in the Northwest, of native descent, a founding member of Kildonan Presbyterian Church. He established the mission c.1873–74 at Riding Mountain amongst his own relatives. His role as certifying witness for Victoria Scott’s 1879 baptism (RG 10\) was family authority, not institutional authority.

\*\*\[V43\] George Flett’s Indigenous ancestry — documented genealogy:\*\* George Flett’s Indigenous descent is now established through documented genealogy rather than inference. His mother Margaret (‘Peggy’) Whitford was the daughter of James Peter Whitford Sr. (London-born HBC employee) and Sarah (Sally), a Saulteaux woman — the same Sarah (Sally) who had previously borne Michael Cardinal (Chief Okanase) by Jacques Cardinal. George Flett’s Indigenous ancestry through the Whitford maternal line is confirmed by the HBCA employee biography and the Louis O’Soup biographical PDF (Métis Museum) \[A\], and independently supported by a contemporary obituary of Mrs. George Flett noting that George Flett possessed Indigenous ancestry (‘some native blood in his veins’). \[A — contemporary obituary\]

Additionally, George Flett married Mary Ross, daughter of Alexander Ross and Sarah (Sally) Timentwa. A tribal research letter identifies Sarah Timentwa’s mother as K’mi’ts’eq and her brother as Abraham Timentwa, placing her within a documented Indigenous kinship network. \[B — tribal research letter\] George Flett therefore sat at the intersection of two documented Indigenous kinship networks: the Cardinal/Whitford line through his mother, and the Ross/Timentwa line through his wife. The Ross family continues to function as a bridge family linking fur trade society, Indigenous kinship networks, Red River society, and western Manitoba settlement communities. \[B\]

The Flett Mission cemetery holds the graves of Keeseekoowenin and his wife, Baptese Bone, Harriet Campbell, and Rev. Flett himself and family. It is the family burial ground of the Okanase / Cardinal network. \[B — Baldy Scott; A — Keeseekoowenin Digital History\]

\#\# \*\*5.13 Reserve Location Sequence — Terrain-Determined\*\*

The location of Riding Mountain Indian Reserve No. 61 near Elphinstone was terrain-determined. The reserve was ultimately established at the terrain node in the Strathclair Road corridor — the same node that attracted the HBC post, the Flett mission, and later the Lauder store. \[A — Keeseekoowenin Digital History / Ringstrom 1981\]

\#\# \*\*5.14 1895 Convergence — End of the Corridor Era\*\*

\[A\] May 1895: HBC Elphinstone Post closed (stock sold to John Lauder for £80 to avoid annual taxes of £275)

\[A\] 1895: Riding Mountain designated as timber reserve (1,535 square miles)

\[A\] 1895: Indian Affairs Annual Report documents Reserve No. 61 in stable, prosperous condition

\[A\] 2 November 1895: Rev. George Flett retired from active mission duty

These four events are the administrative expression of a transition already underway: the fur trade corridor economy giving way to agricultural settler colonialism. The 1930 displacement from Riding Mountain National Park is the final expression of the same process, completed three decades later. Note: the park boundary established by that displacement functions as an inadvertent preservation mechanism. The 1935 eviction of the Keeseekoowenin community created the boundary that now preserves the archaeological evidence of their occupation — including the Kennis Creek clearing. The preservation mechanism and the dispossession are the same event. \[B — geographic interpretation consistent with Parks Canada boundary history\]

\#\# \*\*5.15 The 1889 Post Report — The Corridor’s People Documented Together\*\*

HBCA B.337/e/1, the Riding Mountain Post Report for Outfit 1889 (dated 12–15 February 1889, signed O.H. Beeston), places the full cast of this research simultaneously at the same post. The report confirms the post’s physical setting: ‘The Townsite is adjacent to Okanase Reserve and the situation is central for the small and scattered population of the neighbourhood.’ \[A\]

| \*\*Name\*\* | \*\*Classification in Report\*\* | \*\*Code / Note\*\* |  
| \--- | \--- | \--- |  
| Campbell, A. | Farmer, settler customer list, \~$57 good balance | \[A\] Almost certainly Glenlyon A. Campbell |  
| Campbell, Robt. | Farmer, settler customer list, $129+ | \[A\] Robert Campbell confirmed |  
| Scott, Walter | Labour designation, settler customer list, $16.26 | \[A\] Walter Scott confirmed by name |  
| Flett, Geo. Robt. | Blacksmith, $112.00 | \[A\] Flett family economic integration with post |  
| Skyman, Chief | Indian balance list, 1887 and 1888 columns, $97.01/$97.01 | \[A\] Chief Keeseekoowenin by his Saulteaux name |  
| Ross, Gilbert | Indian balance list, 1887 and 1888 columns | \[A\] Indian accounts section, not settler list; earliest primary-source record at this post |  
| Bone family | Multiple members, Indian balance list | \[A\] Baptese Bone’s extended family confirmed |

\#\# \*\*5.16 Kennis Creek — Named Community and Corridor Residents\*\*

Kennis Creek was a named, occupied social landscape on the corridor before European agricultural settlement arrived in the Big Grass district. The residents documented by Scott Sr. in ‘Some of the People that lived at Kennis Creek’ form a community connected through kinship, HBC trade, and seasonal land use — not a collection of isolated individuals. The creek itself took its name from a resident family. \[A — Scott Sr., Keeseekoowenin First Nation website; A — Our Story to 1970\]

Named residents and their roles in the corridor network:

| \*\*Resident\*\* | \*\*Role / Connection\*\* | \*\*Notes\*\* |  
| \--- | \--- | \--- |  
| Kennis | Trapper, hunter, fur trader | Creek named after him. Married a relative of Okanase. \[A\] \*\*\[V65\]\*\* Independently corroborated — Four Arrows fn 31: Kennis married a relative of Okanase; Okanase’s sister married an Atkinson resident there. Second source for both kinship entries. |  
| Atkinson | Hunter, trapper | Married a sister of Michael Cardinal (Okanase). Direct Cardinal kinship. \[A\] |  
| Jack Johnstone (Chon Cholis) | Farm hand; later Game Warden | Son of Atkinson/Cardinal marriage. Worked for Robert Campbell at Merchiston Ranch. \[A\] |  
| Henry Johnstone | Farm hand | Brother of Jack Johnstone. Also worked for Robert Campbell at Merchiston Ranch. \[A\] Possible connection to Thomas MacDonald Johnstone (Tom Johnstone) — open flag. |  
| John Charles Johnstone | Son of Jack Johnstone | Born Poplar Point, 1870\. His daughter Agnes Johnstone married Frank Hyde of Elphinstone. Frank Hyde was among the purchasers when Riding Mountain Ranch was sold at the outbreak of WWI, connecting the Kennis Creek / Johnstone family directly to the ranch disposal. \[A — Our Story to 1970\] |  
| Joe Mentuck | Hunter, trapper | Family at Kennis Creek Crossing. Helped Campbell/Vivian ranch with hay and stock. \[A\] |  
| Mrs Joe Mentuck | Healer and midwife | Delivered Walter Scott’s first child at Big Grass. \[A\] |  
| Maneseese | Hunter, trapper | Lived at Kennis Creek Crossing alongside the Mentuck family. \[A\] |  
| Jim Audy | Fur trader | Lived where Jackfish Creek flows into Lake Audy. Later took charge of the HBC post at Elphinstone after the Riding Mountain House post relocated. Gave his name to the Audy Trail. \[A\] |

\# \*\*6. CAMPBELL SETTLEMENT PHASE\*\*

\#\# \*\*6.1 Robert Campbell — Corridor Knowledge\*\*

Robert Campbell (1808–1894) arrived in the Swan River District as HBC Chief Factor in autumn 1858 — confirmed by formal transfer escort documented in HBCA B.159/c/1, fo.42, September 26 1858\. \[A\] He commanded the district until his dismissal in 1871\.

The Riding Mountain House at Lake Audy was a subpost of Fort Ellice. HBCA B.337/z/1 (1869): Additional Requisition on Fort Ellice for goods for the Riding Mountain House, Outfit 1869\. \[A\] HBCA B.337/z/2 (1882): Riding Mountain grist mill operational, machinery requisitioned by post manager David Cornish. \[A\] The three documents give a primary-source arc: active Indian trade subpost (1869) → functioning agricultural service centre with mill (1882) → post under commercial pressure (1889) → closure (1895). \[A\]

Robert Campbell returned to Canada in 1873 (age 65 — confirmed in Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021). \[B\] He purchased land near Riding Mountain in 1878 — the year the HBC moved the Riding Mountain House post down the Minnedosa River. He was not choosing an unfamiliar landscape. He was settling at the geographic node he already knew as a district command centre.

Additional biographical notes: Campbell was elected a Fellow of the Royal Geographical Society in 1872\. \[A — RGS records\] He was the first person recorded as importing West Highland cattle to Manitoba; in the 1890s a portion of the herd was sold to the Bar U Ranch in Alberta, where the animals were crossed with shorthorns. \[A — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\] \*\*\[V64\]\*\* An 1885 newspaper advertisement (“Pure Bred West Highland Bulls For Sale”) corroborates and dates the Highland operation: signed jointly “R. & Glenlyon A. Campbell, Merchiston, Strathclair P.O., Man.,” it documents father and son as named principals in a single breeding enterprise, with stock imported from Scottish West Highland herds, reared unhoused at Merchiston Ranche, and marketed for hardiness and beef crossing \[A\]. The joint signature is contemporaneous with the 1885 NWR year and one year after Glen’s Big Grass settlement. The Merchiston operation is the documented district source consistent with the purebred Highland herd observed at Big Grass in 1889; the stock-transfer link itself is held at \[B\], as the advertisement does not document physical movement between the two properties. After the original manuscript of his memoirs was destroyed in the Montreal fire, Glen Campbell persuaded his father to rewrite them; the result was Campbell of the Yukon (Wilson, 1883). \[B — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\]

\*\*\[V65\] Bryce 1901 biography and Crown patent portfolio:\*\* Rev. Dr. Bryce’s 1901 career account (“A Great Scottish Fur Trader”) independently corroborates the Merchiston name and Riding Mountain location, adds Kildonan as burial place (compatible with the Elphinstone death place), records the “Glenlyon House” naming precedent at Simpson’s Tower (1837), and describes annual/semi-annual Winnipeg trips from Merchiston \[A — Bryce direct recollection\]. Bryce’s 1863 marriage date and “Edinburgh” death place for Elleonora are demonstrable errors against E.48/7; standing note: Bryce alone promotes nothing to \[A\]. Crown patents now held in image \[A\]: SE¼ S34 T18 R21 W1 (the Riding Mountain House site — see Section 6.3); Westbourne Settlement lots 17 & 18 (392 acres, 21 Nov 1883\) and the easterly six chains of lot 27 (19 Jun 1886), White Mud River frontage — river-lot holdings at the overland corridor’s southern staging point, a new landscape-network fact. Four Minnedosa-corridor patents in a resident-farmer styling are held unattributed; four others are excluded as different Robert Campbells (see V65 integration note).

\#\# \*\*6.2 Elleonora Campbell — The Post Household\*\*

Elleonora C. Stirling Campbell (1837–1871, Comrie / Clathick, Monzievaird, Perthshire) was sent from Scotland to marry Robert Campbell at Norway House on 5 August 1859\. \[A — E.48/7\] She managed Fort Chipewyan during Robert’s six-month absence and maintained recognisable Scottish household culture across multiple postings. She died of typhoid fever in Scotland on 24 August 1871\. \[A — E.48/7\]

Note: Archibald Stirling was Elleonora’s brother — Glen’s maternal uncle. He was living in Scotland while Glen attended school in Edinburgh and acted as a surrogate father to Glen. Glen later received correspondence claiming to be the sole beneficiary of Archibald Stirling’s Australian estate (claimed value $8 million). Glen was sceptical of the claim: ‘I am not carried away exactly with my chances.’ He never obtained the inheritance. \[B — Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021; A — Victoria British Colonist, 1912\]

Naming note: Elleonora is the confirmed spelling per E.48/7 in Campbell’s own hand. All prior variants (Elenora / Eleanora / Eleanor) are superseded.

\#\# \*\*6.3 The Riding Mountain Ranch — Founding Sequence\*\*

| \*\*Stage\*\* | \*\*Date\*\* | \*\*Event\*\* | \*\*Source\*\* |  
| \--- | \--- | \--- | \--- |  
| 1 | 1873 | Robert Campbell returned to Canada | Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021 \[B\] |  
| 2 | 1878 | Robert Campbell purchased land near Riding Mountain | PAM finding aid \[A\] |  
| 3 | 1880 | Buildings erected on property — Merchiston Ranch in operation | Bryce / E.48/3 \[B\]; Ringstrom 1981 \[A\] |  
| 4 | Oct 1884 | Glen Campbell patented fractional E½ S3 T19 R21 W1 (314 acres) — family land in the Riding Mountain/Elphinstone block | Crown patent image, 7 Oct 1884 \[A\] \*\*\[V65\]\*\* |  
| 5 | 1883 | Lord Elphinstone visited and purchased 13 sections of land encompassing the new town of Elphinstone | Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021 \[B\] |  
| 6 | 1885 | Merchiston Ranche operating under joint R. & Glenlyon A. Campbell signature (Highland-cattle advertisement) | 1885 advertisement \[A\] |  
| 7 | 27 Aug 1887 | Dominion Lands homestead filing for SE¼ S.34 T18 R21 — the vacated Riding Mountain House site | Neepawa LTO Doc 55619 (per Four Arrows fn 29\) \[B\]; Keeseekoowenin Digital History \[B\] |  
| 8 | 1887/88 | Crown patent issued to Robert Campbell for SE¼ S34 T18 R21 W1 (“of the City of Winnipeg, Gentleman”) | Crown patent image \[A\] \*\*\[V65\]\*\* |  
| 9 | c.1897–98 | Glen sold or mortgaged Merchiston to fund the Klondike expedition; Lord Vivian became owner; renamed Riding Mountain Ranch (operated 1900–16, Walter Scott Sr. managing) | Baldy Scott, “Riding Mountain Ranch 1900–1916” \[B\] \*\*\[V65\]\*\* |

\*\*\[V65\] R20/R21 discrepancy RESOLVED — retired.\*\* The Crown patent instrument reads Range Twenty-first — R21, matching the 1875 Sinclair DLS survey. The Keeseekoowenin Digital History R20 citation (and the Four Arrows main-text R20, against its own fn 29\) are transcription errors. Robert Campbell’s homestead patent covers the site of the abandoned Riding Mountain House itself; the framework’s existing corridor-knowledge insight (Section 6.1) is thereby literal — he patented the actual post ground. Traill’s 1868 journal (via Four Arrows) states the rebuilt post stood “where the Merchiston Ranch was to be located.” Note: the 1885 operating advertisement predates the 1887 filing — the post-quarter filing was an addition to an already-operating ranch, not its founding.

\#\# \*\*6.4 Glenlyon Archibald Campbell (1863–1917) — Complete Biography\*\*

Born 23 October 1863, Fort Pelly (now Saskatchewan). Educated at Glasgow Academy and Merchiston Castle School, Edinburgh. Described as over six feet tall, dark and handsome. \[B — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\] Approximately two years in Montana learning cattle ranching. Arrived Big Grass district fall 1884\. First non-Indigenous settler at Gilbert Plains. \[A — multiple sources\] At the Riel Rebellion of 1885 he was the only man given a field promotion to Captain. \[B — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\] He was known for rising in the House of Commons when bored and delivering his response entirely in Cree sprinkled with Latin. \[B — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\]

Klondike expedition: departed Edmonton 1898 (REVISED from project’s 1897 — Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021 states 1898; Calgary Herald March 15, 1900 corroborates 1898 departure). Led twelve friends. Named party members in photograph: W.J.K. Osbourne, Ernie Read, Frank Black, George Black, Walter Scott, Charles Macdonald, Alex Mclean, Frank Bedingfield, Bouvette, Thomas Taylor, and two unnamed Indigenous men. The expedition was forced to turn back before reaching the Klondike. \[B — Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021\] NOTE: The claim that Campbell’s party was ‘the only one to successfully complete the journey’ is RETIRED pending source identification. The book explicitly states the party turned back. \[V63 ADDITION\] A contemporary notice (Free Press Prairie Farmer, 16 Dec 1897, reprinted from the Minnedosa Tribune) reports the expedition as being organized in December 1897 for a planned departure “about the beginning of February” 1898 via Edmonton and Peace River — consistent with, not contradicting, the existing V33 revision to an 1898 departure \[A\]. The same notice adds that Robert Campbell (Glen’s father, of Elphinstone, already on record via the HBC E.48 papers) was among the first white men to visit the Klondike gold districts, discovered gold there but did not pursue the find given the district’s remoteness at the time, and reportedly left a chart of the discovery site which Glen hoped to use to locate a claim \[A\]. Walter Scott Sr. is confirmed as Glen’s partner in organizing the 1898 expedition, securing dogs and arrangements \[A\].

Post-Klondike: Walter Scott managed the Riding Mountain Ranch for Lord Vivian 1900–1916. Glen was elected MLA for Gilbert Plains 1903 (elected Conservative; acclaimed 1907 after two unsuccessful attempts in 1892 and 1896 against Theodore A. Burrows; resigned 1908). MP for Dauphin federal riding 1908; defeated 1911 by Robert Cruise. \[A — Manitoba Historical Society (MHS) — Memorable Manitobans\] \*\*\[V64\]\*\* The 1892 campaign is now corroborated in the contemporary press: a Winnipeg Tribune report (16 Jun 1892, p.4) documents Campbell as Opposition candidate for Dauphin at a 10 June 1892 public meeting at Gartmore, Lake Dauphin (\~150 voters; chair W. Adam of Adam & Co.; Dr. Beauchamp and H.C. Ross speaking for Campbell), with Burrows speaking for the government side — confirming the MHS Burrows identification from a primary source. Campaign issues recorded: the school question, N.P.R. freight rates, Greenway-government economy \[A — factual skeleton; partisan characterizations excluded\]. Appointed Chief Inspector of Indian Affairs for the West, 1912\. \[A — Manitoba Historical Society (MHS) — Memorable Manitobans\] Director of Ceremonies, Robbie Burns Lodge No. 103, Gilbert Plains, instituted 9 March 1905\. Indian Agent (Indian Agency). Recruited and commanded the 107th (Timber Wolf) Battalion WWI — approximately 500 of 860 men Indigenous; A Company 100% Indigenous (except NCOs). 107th arrived Vimy March 1917 with 45 officers and 860 other ranks. Departed Folkestone February 25, 1917 aboard the Olympic for France. \[A/B — multiple sources\]

Final illness and death: Became critically ill from sepsis caused by chronic kidney disease. Removed from the front October 9, 1917\. Died October 20, 1917, France. Toronto Star (October 22, 1917): ‘His death occurred in a hospital in France from kidney trouble.’ \[A\] Last letter: October 2, 1917, to Dauphin newspaper, longing to return to his ranch and live the remainder of his life in peace and quiet. \[A — corroborated by Dauphin Herald\]

Grant MacEwan on Glen Campbell (Fifty Mighty Men, Western Producer, Prairie Books, 1975): ‘On the surface, Glen was a rip-roaring, wild man from Borneo, kind of man, but his heart was as big as an ox.’ \[A — published secondary source\]

\#\#\# \*\*2.5 The Receiving Node Reframe (V48)\*\*

\*\*Previous model:\*\* Gilbert Plains was a destination — a farming district where settlers arrived and established themselves independently after navigating the immigration system.

\*\*Revised model:\*\* Gilbert Plains was a \*receiving node\* — a community with documented human connections to the British immigration recruitment and placement infrastructure, functioning as an organized receiving point within a coordinated end-to-end system.

\*\*Evidence base for reframe \[A/B\]:\*\*

The following independent evidence streams converge on this interpretation:

\- Thomas Embleton (Gilbert Plains) conducted a documented Farm Delegate recruitment mission to Britain (1907), file number 593252, nominated by T.A. Burrows \[A\]

\- Thomas Parsons (Glenlyon, immediately adjacent district) nominated as a Farmer Delegate by Burrows \[A\]

\- A.F. Jury visited the Lake Dauphin district in 1904 with Hanna and Burrows (Lake Manitoba Railway) actively assisting — the district was a named receiving target within the Immigration Branch at least seven years before Alfred’s arrival \[A\]

\- The Carbert family was established at Section 21 T24 R22W1 by 1894 — seventeen years before Alfred’s placement there \[A\]

\- Alfred’s manifest names Gilbert Plains directly before sailing — under the documented pre-placed scheme this indicates a contractually fixed destination, not self-directed travel \[A\]

\- A second Gilbert Plains-bound farmer travelled on the same Tunisian voyage (ticket 22163\) \[A\]

\- Theodore A. Carbert (corrected from earlier “Theodora” reading, V61) is recorded as Foreman, own account, with labourers in his household — a managed agricultural operation, not a simple family farm \[A\]

\- Alfred William Knott holds ¾ of Section 21 T24 R22W1 by 1923 — the same section where he was placed as a labourer in June 1911 \[A\]

\*\*Interpretive status:\*\* \[B\] — The receiving node characterization is a well-supported inference from multiple convergent \[A\] sources. It is not yet confirmed by a single primary source explicitly describing Gilbert Plains as a designated receiving district. That confirmation, if it exists, would most likely be found in RG76 Volume 362 (Manitoba-side receiving agent records) or in delegate correspondence files not yet examined.

\*\*Reframe implications for prior sections:\*\*

This reframe does not invalidate earlier findings. It recontextualizes them. The Transportation Corridor Model (Section 4), the settlement pattern evidence (Section 9), and the Campbell/Knott convergence (Section 11\) all remain intact. What changes is the interpretive frame: the question is no longer “why did Alfred end up in Gilbert Plains?” but “why was Alfred routed into a community with documented links to the immigration placement machinery?”

\*\*Primary outstanding research question:\*\* Identification of the district-level receiving actor(s) operating between Winnipeg reception and Gilbert Plains farm placement. Current candidates: Thomas Parsons, Thomas Embleton, Theodore Burrows, district receiving committees, church networks, agricultural societies. Most likely surviving record type: delegate operational reports, receiving-farm registrations, or district agent correspondence in RG76.

\#\#\# \*\*6.4.1 Glen’s Marriages and Family — Complete Record\*\*

First marriage: Harriet Burns Campbell (c.1863–1910 — see Section 6.5). Married 1 April 1886 at Keeseekoowenin Reserve. Harriet died 17 May 1910 at Elphinstone.

\*\*\[V66 ADDITION\] Death-place harmonization:\*\* “At Elphinstone” (above) and the Section 6.5 account of death at the Riding Mountain Ranch are read as registration-district vs. physical location, not competing claims; per the V65 Merchiston/Riding Mountain Ranch identity finding, family memory that Harriet “died at Merchiston” and the Settlers of the Plains ranch account are the same statement. \[B\]

\*\*\[V66 ADDITION\] Will, probate, and estate — primary instruments \[A\]:\*\* Glen’s complete Surrogate Court probate file (Eastern Judicial District, 16 images, FamilySearch/PAM IG 005335575\) and CEF service-file pages (LAC RG150) are integrated. Will executed 12 Feb 1917 in the 107th Bn (two independent copies: handwritten P.85 probate exhibit; typed M.F.W. 82 service-file copy — the typed copy retires a 1916/1917 handwritten-date ambiguity). Witnesses: Lieut. Hubert J. McCusker and John William Waddy, soldier, 107th Bn — Christina’s husband, serving in Glen’s battalion. Real estate to the four children of the first marriage; personal estate equally divided except BoM Winnipeg monies to Mary Cameron Campbell and BoM Waterloo Place, London monies to Lillian Ellwood (lately 107 Wentworth Block, Winnipeg; “now at H.O. London” — new open name, identity unknown; “Home Office” expansion held \[C\]). Florence and Hugh appear in neither copy — the exclusion is structural in the instrument; the DCB remains the sole source for the children-persuaded-him explanation. No executor named; the four children renounced probate 17 July 1918 (witness Bruce Brown; sworn before Duncan Cameron, Commissioner) in favour of Union Trust Co. Ltd.; letters of administration 12 Dec 1918; duty $232.85; solicitors Tupper, McTavish, Foley & Tupper. Cause of death documented for the first time: calculus ureter and toxaemia, No. 20 General Hospital, France (Militia & Defence certificate, 27 Nov 1918); Étaples (probate affidavit) vs. Camiers (DCB) reconciled by the hospital’s location in the Étaples/Camiers cluster \[B\]. The Affidavit of Survivors (13 Nov 1918\) lists the four children plus Florence Campbell (wife) and “H. H. Campbell” (son) — the first primary-source record of Hugh (second initial new, unexplained). Inventory $29,453.25; land findings at Section 6.5 below.

\*\*\[V66 ADDITION\] Florence Campbell — statutory track and relocation \[A — LAC RG150 pay cards\]:\*\* Glen’s enlistment confirmed 3 Jan 1916\. Florence documented as wife-beneficiary; address sequence 210 Maryland St. → 619 Sherbrooke St. → 204 Good St., Winnipeg → c/o Imperial Bank, Victoria, B.C.; separation allowance $60/mo; assignment $75 from 1 Nov 1917; widow’s pension granted effective 1 Jan 1918\. The statutory track ran independently of the will’s exclusion. The Tupper letter attests Glen was married before going overseas — with the 3 Jan 1916 enlistment this brackets the Wesley marriage documentarily, consistent with DCB’s 1916 \[B pending marriage registration\]. The V61 Saskatchewan 1921-census candidate is closed as ruled out (wrong province and trajectory); Florence’s post-1918 search anchor is Victoria, B.C.

\*\*\[V67 ADDITION\] Ellwood identification and Florence chronology note \[B — Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021, uncited passage\]:\*\* Glen III’s book identifies Lillian Ellwood as Glen’s personal nurse, with whom he began a relationship before going overseas; she accompanied him to England and remained there for the war. This contextualizes the V66 Ellwood bequest without promoting her identity above \[B\] (the passage carries no source citation). Florence is identified as Glen’s secretary during his time with the Indian Agency \[B\]. The same passage dates the marriage “three years after Harriet’s death” (c.1913) and states it “ended in 1915” — both contradicted by the V66 primary record (Florence styled wife-beneficiary from Glen’s 3 Jan 1916 enlistment through the 1918 Affidavit of Survivors and 1918 pension grant); not adopted. A possible informal-separation reconciliation (c.1915–16, coinciding with the Ellwood relationship) is logged at \[C\] only. Standing source-reliability note: Glenlyon Campbell III (2021) remains a strong prosopographical secondary source generally, but this specific second-marriage chronology is unreliable against primary instruments — analogous to the existing Bryce reliability note (Section 5).

\*\*\[V56 ADDITION\] Civil marriage record \[A\]:\*\* The Manitoba Marriage Index (vitalstats.gov.mb.ca) records a \*\*civil\*\* marriage of Glenlyon A. Campbell and Harriet Burns on \*\*24 August 1891, Strathclair, Manitoba\*\*. This is a separate, later civil registration from the 1 April 1886 Keeseekoowenin Reserve date above. Family information holds that Glen and Harriet had two ceremonies — one at the reserve, one at Strathclair several years later \[B — oral tradition, source not yet identified despite being stated in multiple places; do not promote to \[A\] without a named source\]. The civil date is difficult to explain otherwise: Margaret Campbell’s birth (26 Nov 1885), Jack Campbell’s birth (23 Feb 1889), and the household’s recorded “Married” status at the June 1891 census all predate the August 1891 civil registration. The two-ceremony reading is accordingly logged at \[B\] — a well-supported inference from the convergence of these primary sources — rather than \[A\], since no document of the reserve ceremony itself has been located.

Second marriage: Florence Wesley. Florence was Glen’s secretary during his time with the Indian Agency. One child from this union (Hugh Campbell). \[B — Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021\] \*\*\[V56\]\*\* Glen Campbell (correspondent, great-great-grandson) confirms no information has been located on Hugh Campbell from the living-family side either — the gap is now confirmed from both the archival and family-memory directions, not merely an absence of records. Remains open as a research target.

\*\*\[V61 ADDITION\] Dictionary of Canadian Biography confirmation \[A\]:\*\* The DCB entry for Archibald Glenlyon Campbell (Desmond Morton, vol. 14, University of Toronto/Université Laval, accessed 11 July 2026\) confirms the marriage date as \*\*1916\*\* (not circa 1913 as previously logged from family-oral sourcing) and independently confirms one son from the union. Florence is described in this scholarly source as “a young Englishwoman living in Winnipeg” at the time of the marriage — the first geographic anchor point for her outside the Campbell family network. The DCB further confirms, from an independent scholarly source rather than family oral tradition, the will exclusion: “To his family’s dismay, he married a young Englishwoman living in Winnipeg. The children of his first marriage persuaded their father to exclude her from his will.” This upgrades the marriage date to \[A\] (superseding the earlier circa-1913 \[B\] estimate) and upgrades the will-exclusion detail from unsourced family claim to \[A\]. The 1915 marriage-end date previously logged is not corroborated by this source and should be treated with caution pending its own primary confirmation — the DCB does not state that the marriage ended, only that Florence was excluded from Glen’s will (which he could have done independent of any separation, and which is consistent with the marriage continuing until his October 1917 death). \[A — Dictionary of Canadian Biography, https://www.biographi.ca/en/bio/campbell\_archibald\_glenlyon\_14E.html\]

\*\*\[V61 ADDITION\] Search for Florence Wesley/Campbell in Canadian records — two candidates checked, neither adopted \[C\]:\*\* (1) A Form 30 passenger declaration for Florence May Campbell, destined Winnipeg, husband named Ernest Campbell — ruled out on husband-name grounds, unconnected to Glen. (2) A 1921 Census of Canada record, Saskatchewan, District 223 (Prince Albert), RM of Weldon: Florence Campbell, domestic servant in the household of Tom Lucey, age 30 (b. c.1891), arrived Canada 1902, marital status recorded “single.” The birth-year window is plausible for the DCB-confirmed 1916 marriage, but the “single” status is difficult to reconcile with the DCB’s account: by 1921 Florence would be a widow of a decorated WWI officer, and the Winnipeg-based description in the DCB does not obviously connect to a rural Saskatchewan domestic-servant record with no apparent link to the Manitoba Campbell/Indian Agency network. Logged as open, weighted against adoption, not ruled out. \[C\]

Relationship with Lillian Ellwood: Before going overseas, Glen began a relationship with his personal nurse, Lillian Ellwood. She accompanied him to England and remained there for the duration of the war. \[B — Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021\] New figure, not previously in the project record.

\#\#\# \*\*6.4.2 Glen’s Children — Complete Set\*\*

From Dauphin Herald death notice (October 22, 1917\) — surviving children at death: (1) Mrs. Christina Campbell Waddy; (2) Mrs. Elleonora (Nora) Campbell Knott of Gilbert Plains; (3) Mary Campbell \[Austin\]; (4) Private Jack Campbell (184th / 107th Battalions). \[A\]

\*\*\[V56 ADDITION\] Margaret Campbell (1885–1894) — confirmed \[A\]:\*\* A fifth child is established by convergence of three independent sources: the Manitoba Birth Index (female “Unnamed Campbell,” born 26 Nov 1885, R.M. of Strathclair, mother Harriet Burns); the 1891 Census of Canada (Manitoba, Marquette, Shoal Lake and Strathclair, enumerated 6 June 1891), which lists the household of Glenlyon A. Campbell (27, M) and Harriet (27, Wife) with children Margaret (5, Daughter), John R. (3, Son), and Nora (1/12, Daughter); and family tree data giving Margaret Campbell, 1885–1894. Margaret’s absence from the 1917 Dauphin Herald “surviving children” notice above, and from the MHS Memorable Manitobans “four children” summary (Section 6.4), is explained by her death in 1894 at about age 9 — both of those sources are framed around children living at the time of reference, not a complete birth list. This is logged as a resolved predecease, not a contradiction. The 1891 census also independently corroborates Nora’s existing 14 April 1891 birthdate (recorded as 1/12, i.e. about one month old, at the 6 June 1891 enumeration) rather than conflicting with it. \*\*Resolved \[A\]:\*\* Nora’s birthdate stands at 14 April 1891 — confirmed by RG 10 (Section 11.3), the gravestone (1891–1975, Section 11.3), and the 1891 census age entry above. The MHS Memorable Manitobans bio’s “1890” is a secondary compiled-source error, outweighed by three independent primary-source confirmations; no further action required. Mary Campbell’s married name was Austin (confirmed Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021). Previously listed as ‘married name unresolved.’ \[CLOSED\]

Thomas MacDonald “Tom” Johnstone (1889–1939) — Glen had an additional son from a prior relationship. Existence, full name, and dates confirmed: \[A — Manitoba Historical Society (MHS) — Memorable Manitobans, verified directly against the MHS source page rather than taken on a relayed quote\]. Relationship to Glen as son from a prior relationship, battalion service (79th Battalion, transferring to the 107th), role as Winnipeg police officer, and Canadian champion wrestler status: \[B — Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021 — requires corroboration\]. Note: Henry Johnstone appears in the project record as a Big Grass farm hand; possible family connection unresolved.

\*\*\[V56 ADDITION\] Oral history corroboration \[B\]:\*\* Glen Campbell (email, June 2026\) provides direct descendant testimony of an affair between his great-grandfather (Glenlyon Archibald Campbell) and “his father’s housekeeper” — i.e. employed by Glen’s own father — and states that Thomas Johnstone and Jack Campbell were born about a month apart. This is independently convergent with the existing documentary record: Tom Johnstone b. 19 Jan 1889 vs. Jack Campbell b. 23 Feb 1889 — approximately five weeks apart. This is logged as new family-oral corroboration strengthening the oral-tradition side of the paternity question; it does not resolve the \[C\] paternity tension below, since the birth registration still names John Johnstone, not Glenlyon Campbell, as father. \*\*Correction flag:\*\* a previously circulated restated detail — that Tom Johnstone was “present at his father’s death” — is incorrect and should not be propagated. That is Jack Campbell’s documented experience (bearing the burden of Glen’s death at Passchendaele, Section 7.1), not Tom Johnstone’s; Tom’s record is enlistment, transfer, and demobilization only.

\*\*\[V52 addition\]\*\* Cross-referenced against two further primary sources. Manitoba Birth Index: Thomas Johnstone, born 19 January 1889, R.M. of Strathclair, mother Margaret McDonald \[A\]. CEF service record: Thomas McDonald Johnstone of Gilbert Plains, Teamster, married, next of kin Lilly Johnstone; enlisted July 1915 into the 79th Battalion, transferred into the 107th Battalion structure, served in France and Belgium, promoted to Sergeant, demobilized 28 April 1919 \[A\]. The attestation paper gives a birth date of 19 July 1888 and birthplace Gilbert Plains, against the birth index’s 19 January 1889 and R.M. Strathclair — a six-month date discrepancy and a birthplace discrepancy, carried forward as one open item rather than two \[open flag\].

Critically, the original birth registration names John Johnstone — not Glenlyon Campbell — as father \[A\]. This is a direct primary-source tension with the MHS paternity statement above: identity across the MHS entry, birth index, and CEF record converges strongly on one individual, but that convergence does not by itself resolve who his biological father was. The MacDonald middle name follows the standard pattern of a mother’s maiden surname being preserved in an unmarried child’s name, which is consistent with Margaret McDonald either already being married to, or shortly partnered with, a John Johnstone — but it does not establish whether John Johnstone or Glenlyon Campbell was the biological father. Tom’s own son with Lilly Johnstone carries Glen as a middle name, which is logged as circumstantial \[B\] support for an enduring family belief in or connection to Glenlyon Campbell — comparable in evidentiary shape to the Tom Knott “White Feather” disclosure pattern already documented in Section 8, where the official record and the family’s private understanding diverge — but a middle name alone is not proof of paternity, since children were also named after political and military figures admired without blood relation. \*\*Net evidence position: parentage downgraded from \[B\] to \[C\].\*\* The previously-flagged Henry Johnstone connection (above) should now be checked specifically against this John Johnstone — same person misrecorded, father and son, or unrelated namesakes are all live possibilities. The single highest-value next document is the original Manitoba birth registration itself (not the index entry), which may carry an informant name or residency notation the index does not show. A separate hypothesis — that Margaret McDonald (Tom’s mother) and Margaret Johnstone (Section 11.5.13, V48 WWI postcard recipient, previously logged as an unplaced name) are the same woman — is strengthened by a detail already on record but not previously connected to this question: the same Facebook source names Rose Holt specifically as Thomas’s granddaughter, places Thomas and Jack Campbell serving together in the 107th, and describes the postcard of the two men in uniform as mailed to Margaret Johnstone. That is independent network corroboration, not a coincidence of surname, and the hypothesis is accordingly promoted to \[B\] — Margaret Johnstone is most plausibly Tom’s mother, the postcard a wartime image of her son and his half-brother sent home to her. It is not yet \[A\]: no document directly states Margaret Johnstone was born McDonald or married a Johnstone, so the link remains an inference rather than a confirmed identity, and a McDonald–Johnstone marriage record naming John Johnstone is still the document that would close it.

\#\#\# \*\*6.4.3 The 107th Battalion Connection\*\*

Three members of the Campbell family network served in the 107th: (1) Lieutenant Colonel Glen Campbell D.S.O. — Commander; (2) Lieutenant John Robert (Jack) Campbell — Glen’s son, enlisted 184th, transferred 107th, repatriated January 1918 with shell shock; (3) John William Waddy (Jack Waddy) — Glen’s son-in-law (husband of Christina), Quarter Master Sergeant, Boer War veteran with Lord Strathcona’s Horse Regiment. Thomas Johnstone (Glen’s earlier son) served in the 79th / 107th. \[A/B — multiple sources\]

James Allan Yuill, Gilbert Plains, 107th Battalion: author of the poem ‘A Man of Worth’ (published Grandview Exponent, 1919). New local history figure to add to district record.

\*\*\[V58 ADDITION\] Battalion composition \[A — DCB; CMEA; CBC; APTN\]:\*\* The 107th was one of only two purpose-built Indigenous battalions in the Canadian Expeditionary Force — the other being the 114th “Brock’s Rangers” (Six Nations). Approximately 500 of 860–1,000 men in the 107th were Indigenous, drawn from across western Canada. Glen Campbell raised the battalion on the explicit basis of his reputation among and relationship with Indigenous communities; he argued to his superiors that keeping Indigenous soldiers together under Indigenous-familiar leadership would protect them from the racism they would face in mixed units, and fought successfully to prevent the battalion’s dissolution in England. The 107th was redesignated a Pioneer Battalion in January 1917 — allowing Campbell to keep his men together while meeting the operational demand for pioneer units in France.

Recruitment method: Glen Campbell obtained authorization from Duncan Campbell Scott, Deputy Superintendent General of Indian Affairs, to enrol underage males directly from the residential schools at Brandon and Elkhorn, Manitoba. \[A — Dictionary of Canadian Biography\] This recruiting source connects directly to the residential-school stories documented in Aunt Mary’s testimony (Section 8.7) — the same institutions that reserve-connected cousins described to Mary Campbell as places where children suffered abuse are confirmed as a primary recruitment pool for the battalion her own father and grandfather commanded. See Section 7.2 for the suppression-paradox interpretation this creates.

\#\# \*\*6.5 Harriet Burns Campbell — Scrip Selection as Geographic Literacy\*\*

Harriet Burns (10 April 1866 — birth date from Descendancy register; \~1863 in earlier project versions — 17 May 1910, Elphinstone) married Glen Campbell 1 April 1886 at Keeseekoowenin Reserve. Her Métis scrip selection — NW¼ \+ N½SW¼ of Section 1, T24 R22W1, 240 acres, Crown Grant 1895 — placed her holding at the escarpment corridor exit, the same parcel where Ross’s cabin had stood. \[A — scrip records; A — 1890 DLS survey\] \*\*\[V65\]\*\* The patent instrument itself is now held in image: grant to Harriet Campbell, styled “of Elphinstone … Wife of Glenlyon Archibald Campbell,” NW¼ and N½ of SW¼ of Section One, T24 R22 W1, 240 acres, dated 17 June 1895, with a marginal N.W.H.B. grant notation flagged for cross-check against the scrip mechanism \[A — Crown patent image\]. Glen’s own patent for S½ S12 of the same township (320 acres, 15 Oct 1901 \[A — Crown patent image\]) adjoins immediately north — the family block at the corridor exit comprised two contiguous patented parcels totalling 560 acres. A photograph of the sod-roofed Ross cabin on the Campbell ranch, circa 1897, is confirmed in Glenlyon Campbell III (2021), p.75, explicitly captioned as Glen’s accommodation during ranch house construction. \[B — family memoir\]

\*\*\[V66 ADDITION\] Estate land holdings and Harriet devolution \[A — inventory; B — devolution reading\]:\*\* Glen’s 1918 estate inventory records a ⅓ interest in W½ S1 T24 R22 WPM ($4,000) and the sale of E½ S1 to J.J. Drinkwater (agreement for sale, 30 June 1916, $4,000 at 8%). With Harriet predeceasing Glen (1910), the ⅓ interest is consistent with a devolved share of her scrip parcel (NW¼ \+ N½SW¼ S1 lies within W½ S1), the remaining shares plausibly with the children — completing the chain Harriet (1895 patent) → partial devolution incl. Glen’s ⅓ (1910–17) → J.R. Campbell on the 1923 Cummins map. Held at \[B\] (\[C\] for the ⅓ arithmetic) pending a Land Titles abstract for S1 T24 R22 (new Tier 2 target). Open items: E½ S1 lies outside Harriet’s patent and Glen’s acquisition of it is undocumented; Glen’s own S½ S12 patent parcel (1901) is absent from the estate entirely; the estate also held NW¼ and Fr. SW¼ S19 T56 R26 WPM — a northern Manitoba holding not previously in the framework — and a second mortgage on N½ S8 T24 R23 (mortgagor George Rutledge). Additional estate detail: SE¼ S2 T24 R22 ($4,500); livestock and produce; cash at Sterling Bank Gilbert Plains and BoM Winnipeg.

Death and burial: In early summer 1910, Harriet became ill and was taken to the Riding Mountain Ranch where her sisters could look after her. She died there on 17 May 1910\. \[A — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\] Burial location discrepancy: the RM of Strathclair history records her burial at the Indian Cemetery, Elphinstone; Scott Jr. states the Flett Mission cemetery. The Flett Mission was located on NW¼ 9-18-21W, within the Elphinstone townsite cluster; these may be the same site referred to by different names, or genuinely different locations. \[Open flag — requires resolution\]

\*\*\[V57 ADDITION\] Cemetery location strengthened \[B\]:\*\* \*The White Feather\* describes Tom Knott’s travel route with Glen III and John — through Riding Mountain Park, exiting at its southern boundary directly onto a sign reading “Keeseekoowenin Ojibway First Nation,” then immediately to the cemetery containing Moses Burns and Harriet Burns Campbell. This continuous travel sequence places the cemetery at or on the Keeseekoowenin Reserve itself (Elphinstone area), which fits the RM of Strathclair history’s “Indian Cemetery, Elphinstone” far better than a geographically distant Clear Lake location. This does not fully resolve the discrepancy above — whether the Flett Mission cemetery (NW¼ 9-18-21W) is the same site or a separate one within the same townsite cluster remains open — but it strengthens the Elphinstone reading and rules out a Clear Lake interpretation. \[B — narrative travel-route description, The White Feather\]

Harriet’s application (No. 236,212) was processed in parallel with her sister Victoria’s (File 109,429). Glen Campbell managed the bureaucratic process on Harriet’s behalf, providing the Whitford lineage chain that unblocked the scrip claim in December 1893\. \[A — RG 10\]

Suppression context (V33 addition): Jack Campbell, Harriet and Glen’s son, grew up mostly at the reserve and his first language was Ojibway. He told his own family he was of Scottish descent. He never disclosed that his mother was Ojibway. His father (Glen) forbade disclosure; the family covered the family’s dark complexion with a story of French ancestry. This active suppression of Indigenous identity within the Jack Campbell branch is documented in The White Feather (Glen Campbell, 2024). \[A — The White Feather\] The silence operated in parallel with Jack’s silence about his war experience — two distinct suppressions running across the same lifetime. \[B — interpretive inference\]
