\# \*\*7. JOHN ROBERT (JACK) CAMPBELL (1889–1977) — EXPANDED PROFILE\*\*

\#\# \*\*7.1 Biographical Overview\*\*

Born 23 February 1889, Elphinstone, Manitoba. Died 12 August 1977, Victoria, British Columbia. Glen and Harriet’s son. Enlisted 184th Battalion 1914; transferred to 107th Battalion 1916\. Survived Vimy Ridge, Hill 70, and Passchendaele — bearing the additional burden of his father Glen’s death at Passchendaele. Shell shock onset at Hill 70\. By December 1917 unable to perform duties — night terrors, delirium, weight loss of 30 lbs from a six-foot-five frame. Repatriated January 1918\. Promoted Lieutenant on return. Post-combat medical exam: blind in one eye, limited vision in the other. Glen’s death also precluded Jack from returning to university to complete the pharmacy degree he had started before the war. He never spoke of his war experience; personal effects from the war, except for one photograph, were discarded before his move to Victoria. \[A — LAC Personal Records of the First World War; corroborated by Glen Campbell III (2021)\]

\*\*\[V67 ADDITION\] 107th Battalion contemporary primary sources (via Glenlyon Campbell III, 2021, citations) \[A unless noted\]:\*\* Manitoba Free Press, 18 Aug 1917 — Campbell letter after Vimy (“We came here as a unit, so the 107th will always live”); Vimy casualties 10 killed, 25 wounded; five men tracked after transfer to the 16th Battalion (Tiny Leonard, Scott Parcher, James Randle, Sandy Braid, Colin Dow — only Leonard and Braid wounded at time of writing). Manitoba Free Press, 14 Aug 1918 — cemetery sergeant quoted: “Colonel Campbell must have been a very popular man. I’ve had more inquiries for his grave than any in the cemetery.” Dauphin newspaper letter, 2 Oct 1917 — Campbell reports fatigue, worsening health, longing for home, eighteen days before death; coheres tightly with the documented cause of death (calculus ureter and toxaemia, Section 6.4). Recruitment: late-1914 Indigenous Battalion proposal rejected; Campbell subsequently raised the 79th, then the 107th Battalion — converging with Thomas Johnstone’s CEF record (79th Bn enlistment, July 1915, transferring into the 107th), logged as circumstantial network evidence \[B\] bearing on but not resolving the Section 6.4.2 paternity question. 114th Battalion (Brock’s Rangers) transfers brought Indigenous composition to roughly 500+ soldiers; A Company reportedly entirely Indigenous except NCOs. Family service confirmed together: Jack Campbell, Thomas Johnstone, and John William “Jack” Waddy served alongside Campbell — independently corroborating the V66 will-witness finding for Waddy and supplying his family-usage forename. Maison Blanche graffiti: battalion insignia, Indigenous names and tribal affiliations, including a Timber Wolf cap-badge carving attributed to Walter McNicol (killed the first day of Vimy) \[A soldier identifications; spatial-association interpretation with 16th Bn transfers held \[B\]\]. Vimy: Campbell reportedly refused to begin forward work until respirators arrived \[B, pending War Diary confirmation\]; first casualties from a shell at an ammunition dump; transferred 107th men buried at C40 (Fred Bagulay, Alfred Kirby, Francis Manning, Robert Pattison, Henry McWilliams, Joseph Willerton). Hill 70: continued Pioneer work (roads, tramways, communications, trenches) before combat; volunteer rescue recovered 25 wounded, 30 dead (Oliver Barron and Andrew Anderson received the Military Medal); mustard gas attack injured 80+; casualties 21 killed, 128 wounded, 93 gassed, 2 missing (Canadian Corps \~8,700). Commendations quoted from Lt.-Col. D.M. Osmond and General Currie, cited to the War Diary — exact wording flagged \[C\] for direct verification. Death confirmed 20 Oct 1917 at Étaples, matching the probate affidavit exactly and reinforcing Étaples over DCB’s Camiers; Major Hugh Walkem assumed command 23 Oct 1917 — also Campbell’s 54th birthday, consistent with the CEF attestation birth date \[A, internal coherence check passes\]; posthumous DSO. Indigenous participation documented prosopographically (Kahnawake, Six Nations, Montreal Lake Cree, Lac La Ronge Cree, Riding Mountain/Keeseekoowenin) rather than by aggregate figures — preserved as a framework note. \[C\] items pending War Diary verification: respirator episode wording, Currie/Osmond commendation texts, origin of “Timber Wolves,” choice of “The Campbells are Coming,” ambulance farewell scene source, and the quoted line “This is one of the happiest days of my life.”

Post-war: 1918 with army reserve in Winnipeg. Ran the Gilbert Plains ranch through to circa 1942\. Moved to Victoria, BC in 1942\. Worked in shipyards two years, then 25-year career in carpentry. Died August 12, 1977, Victoria. \[A — photo caption; B — Mary Campbell Lynch 1998 interview\]

\#\# \*\*7.2 Indigenous Identity and Suppression\*\*

Jack Campbell grew up mostly at the Keeseekoowenin Reserve and his first language was Ojibway. His mother Harriet Burns was the daughter of Chief Keeseekoowenin (Moses Burns) and a member of the Saulteaux people. Jack never disclosed his Indigenous ancestry to his own children. He told his family he was of Scottish descent. His father Glen Campbell forbade disclosure of Indigenous ancestry; the family covered the dark complexion of some members with a story of French ancestry. \[A — The White Feather, Glen Campbell, 2024\]

This suppression remained intact across generations within the Jack Campbell branch. The first disclosure within that branch came in summer 1968, when T.S. (Tom) Knott — CN Rail Station Agent at Gilbert Plains — took a young Glen Campbell (great-great-grandson) to the Keeseekoowenin cemetery and showed him the graves of Moses Burns and Harriet Burns Campbell, saying ‘because they were Indians.’ \[A — The White Feather\]

The two silences: Jack’s silence about his war experience (shell shock, combat trauma) and his silence about his Indigenous ancestry are two separate suppressions operating in parallel across the same lifetime. Neither was ever broken by Jack himself. \[B — interpretive inference consistent with post-scrip, post-residential school suppression documented across the framework\]

\#\#\# \*\*Post-1885 Identity Suppression Context \[V42 ADDITION\]\*\*

Contemporary scholarship documents that many Métis families concealed or actively downplayed Indigenous identity following the 1885 Northwest Resistance, sometimes identifying instead as Scottish, French, or French-Canadian. The Indigenous Peoples Atlas of Canada states directly that the stigma of being labelled rebels or traitors, combined with unending racism for being Indigenous, forced many Métis over several generations to hide or deny their identity, calling themselves French, French-Canadian, or Scottish to escape racism and for their own cultural safety. \[A — Indigenous Peoples Atlas of Canada, “Aftermath of 1885,” indigenouspeoplesatlasofcanada.ca; Royal Canadian Geographical Society / AFN / ITK / MNC joint publication\]

The fraudulent administration of Métis scrip reinforced this structural pressure. The vast majority of recipients never kept or received their scrip land, creating a large number of landless, rootless Métis people with additional incentive to present as non-Indigenous in order to participate in the settler economy. \[A — Indigenous Peoples Atlas of Canada, “Aftermath of 1885”\]

This framework does not assert that any specific Campbell family member consciously adopted a Scottish identity because of post-1885 anti-Métis discrimination. What the evidence establishes is: (1) a documented regional phenomenon in which Métis families concealed Indigenous identity and identified as Scottish or French; (2) a family pattern — Jack Campbell raised on reserve, first language Ojibway, told family Scottish descent only, father forbade disclosure — that is consistent with that documented phenomenon; and (3) a plausible structural mechanism. The Campbell family pattern is therefore coded \[B\] — consistent with a documented regional survival strategy, not yet confirmed as a conscious adoption of it.

What the post-1885 context resolves is a long-standing apparent contradiction in the evidence: deep Indigenous connections (Ojibway language retention, reserve upbringing, kinship networks) coexisting with family stories emphasizing Scottish ancestry. These are not contradictory. They are consistent with a family navigating the post-1885 social environment in which Scottish identity was culturally safe and Indigenous identity was not. \[B — inference from confirmed regional context; coded following GPT epistemics review, June 2026\]

\*\*\[V58 ADDITION\] The suppression paradox \[B — interpretive\]:\*\* Glen Campbell is simultaneously documented as the architect of one of the most visible Indigenous-advocacy acts in the Canadian Expeditionary Force — personally raising, commanding, and protecting a battalion of approximately 500 Indigenous soldiers, fighting to keep them together, and publicly championing their abilities and rights — and as the person who forbade his own family from disclosing their Indigenous ancestry. He gave speeches in the House of Commons in Ojibway and Cree on behalf of Indigenous language rights. He married the daughter of Chief Keeseekoowenin. He recruited underage boys from Brandon and Elkhorn residential schools for his battalion — the same schools his own grandchildren’s generation later heard described as places of abuse and family separation. The suppression was not ignorance. It was a deliberate, structurally enforced policy applied at home by a man whose public identity was built on Indigenous alliance. The mechanism that explains this paradox is not yet identified; the paradox itself is established by convergent \[A\] sources. Cross-reference Section 6.4.3 for the battalion record.

\#\# \*\*7.2a Dorothy Mary Turner — Jack Campbell’s Wife\*\*

Dorothy Mary Turner, born Wormleighton, Warwickshire. Wife of Jack Campbell. Wormleighton is approximately 2 miles from Fenny Compton, Warwickshire — the birthplace of Alfred William Knott. \[C — geographic proximity noted; primary source confirmation of parentage chain pending\]

\*\*\[V56 ADDITION\] Identity convergence — upgraded \[C\] → \[B\]:\*\* The Albert Smith biography confirms Dorothy Turner married Jack Campbell. Combined with (a) Jack Campbell already documented in Section 6.4.3 as Glen and Harriet’s legitimate son, (b) the contemporary Glen Campbell (correspondent, great-great-grandson) being a DNA-confirmed descendant of Jack Campbell, and (c) Glen’s own email independently calling Dorothy Turner “my grandmother” — the identity chain is now multiply convergent. This resolves which specific “Jack Campbell” Dorothy married (Glen and Harriet’s son, the 107th Battalion veteran), not the separate Frederick Turner parentage question below, which remains at its own evidence tier pending primary source confirmation.

Photographic evidence (Ancestry, Catherine Rayner collection, posted 26 April 2009, saved by 21 researchers): A photograph captioned ‘taken on the front porch of Glen’s great-grandfather’s house in Manitoba, early 20th century’ identifies right to left at back: Will Knott, Dorothy (nee Turner), Maurice Turner. Two seated women identified as the photographer’s grandfather’s sisters. Child identified as James (Dorothy’s son) holds a bugle in the foreground. \[A — captioned photograph, Ancestry / Catherine Rayner\]

Significance: Will Knott photographed at the Campbell family home in Manitoba alongside Dorothy Turner is direct visual evidence of social connection between the Knott network and the Campbell/Turner household, independent of the Alfred placement mechanism hypothesis. \[A — photograph existence and caption; B — implication for Knott/Campbell social network\] Maurice Turner (Dorothy’s brother) also present, placing two Turner siblings in Manitoba contemporaneously. Catherine Rayner appears to descend through the Campbell line and may hold additional relevant material — direct contact via Ancestry messaging recommended.

Warwickshire Turner network (active research, June 2026): John C Turner (b. 1858, Aston, Birmingham; resident Lower Boddington, Northamptonshire, 1861 census) identified as sibling of Frederick Turner; subsequent census records place family origin at Wormleighton. Lower Boddington is approximately 3 miles from Wormleighton and 8 miles from Fenny Compton. If Frederick Turner is confirmed as Dorothy’s father, the Wormleighton — Fenny Compton — Lower Boddington geographic cluster would connect Dorothy’s family origin directly to Alfred Knott’s birthplace geography. Research ongoing via BMD and census; independent of other trees. \[C — do not promote until Frederick Turner parentage chain confirmed by primary source\]

\#\# \*\*7.3 Jack Campbell’s Sons Killed in WWII\*\*

Two sons killed in WWII: (1) Flight Sergeant Fred Campbell (1920–1943) — air gunner, Lancaster Bomber EE181, shot down Monchengladbach night raid August 31, 1943, parachuted safely but not seen alive again. Buried Rheinburg War Cemetery, Germany. (2) Private Glen Campbell (1914–1944) — Hastings and Prince Edward Regiment, killed by shell explosion at Battle of Rimini, Italian Campaign, September 15, 1944\. Buried Coriano Ridge War Cemetery, Italy. \[B — Mary Campbell Lynch 1998 interview; photographs\]

\*\*\[V58 ADDITION\] 1931 Census — Jack Campbell household, S35 T23 R22W1 \[A — 1931 Census of Canada, Manitoba, Dauphin, sub-district 0036, lines 24–29\]:\*\* The household of John (Jack) Campbell is recorded at House 108, Family 121\. Head: John Campbell, M, Married, age 42, b. Manitoba. Wife: Dorothy, F, Married, age 42, b. England. Children: Glen (son, S, age 16, b. Manitoba), Fred (son, S, age 10, b. Manitoba), Maurice (son, S, age 4, b. Manitoba), Mary (daughter, S, age 13, b. Manitoba). Dwelling owned, value $2,000, 3 rooms, no radio.

Glen (b. back-calculated \~1914–15) and Fred (b. \~1920–21) corroborate existing framework records for Pte. Glen Campbell (1914–1944) and F/Sgt Fred Campbell (1920–1943) without contradiction. Mary’s recorded age of 13 back-calculates to \~1917–18, a 1–2 year variance against the established b. 1919; minor census-age variance, not a contradiction.

Maurice Campbell (b. \~1927) is a previously undocumented son of Jack Campbell and Dorothy Turner. Possible naming connection: Dorothy Turner’s brother Maurice Turner is already documented in the framework (Section 7.2a, Catherine Rayner photograph). \[C — naming-pattern inference; not independently confirmed\]

Location note: S35 T23 R22W1 is immediately south/diagonal of Harriet Burns Campbell’s 1895 scrip parcel (S1 T24 R22W1) — a single section boundary separating them across a township line. This establishes three-point spatial continuity for the Campbell family land axis: 1895 scrip (S1 T24 R22W1) → 1923 Cummins map (S1/S13 cluster, T24 R22W1) → 1931 census (S35 T23 R22W1). The family remained in the same immediate neighbourhood across 36 years. \[B — DLS adjacency inference from \[A\] primary sources\] \# \*\*8. T.S. (TOM) KNOTT — DISCLOSURE VECTOR AND FAMILY FIGURE\*\*

\#\# \*\*8.1 Identification\*\*

Thomas Spencer Knott (Tom Knott), b. 23 October 1949, Gilbert Plains, Manitoba. d. 30 March 2013, Gilbert Plains. Son of John Robert Cameron Knott (1926–2014) and Margaret Joyce Spencer (b. 1926 Dauphin). Grandson of Alfred William Knott and Elleonora (Nora) Campbell Knott. \[A — Descendancy register; The White Feather, 2024\]

\#\# \*\*8.1a Chatsworth Cemetery — Knott Family Burial Confirmed \[V43\]\*\*

The Manitoba Historical Society Historic Sites database confirms that the Knott family is buried at Chatsworth Cemetery, located at N51.12527, W100.48449, Sec 34 T24 R22W, on Almack land. The cemetery was established December 1898\. The name ‘Chatsworth’ derives from the English estate where George Almack previously worked — providing both origin context for Almack and an explanation for the cemetery name. \[A — MHS Gilbert Plains Sites Report, 02 June 2026\] The MHS entry directly confirms Knott family burial at this location.

\#\# \*\*8.2 CN Rail Service\*\*

T.S. Knott worked for CN Rail at Gilbert Plains for approximately 37 years, 1967–2004. Deceased. His exact job title across that period is not yet confirmed; he is identified as a CN Rail agent at the time of his contact with Glen Campbell III, but whether he held the station agent role for the full tenure is unknown. \[A — The White Feather, 2024\] His CN Rail service plaque sits on Stacey Knott’s desk. Stacey was close to him and knew he carried family knowledge.

\*\*\[V60 ADDITION — audit candidate N5\]\*\* Register confirms Tom’s birth date as 23 October 1949 \[A\], which independently checks the 1967–2004 CN Rail service window already on record above: Tom would have been \~18 at the start of that tenure and \~19 at the 1968 cemetery disclosure (Section 8.3) — near the beginning of his CN Rail career, not its end. The framework’s existing figures are internally consistent; the audit flag is noted here to confirm no anachronism exists (i.e., the 37-year total describes his full 1967–2004 career, correctly not attributed to his status as of the 1968 scene).

\*\*\[V57 ADDITION\]\*\* Glen III’s correspondence confirms Tom was, at the time of their acquaintance, caretaker of the Gilbert Plains Museum. \[A — correspondence\] This connects directly to the long-standing Tier 1 research item concerning the Gilbert Plains Museum binders (Section 14.1) — see Section 14.1 for the updated research hypothesis this raises.

\#\# \*\*8.3 The 1968 Cemetery Visit — First Disclosure\*\*

In summer 1968, T.S. Knott introduced a young Glen Campbell (a member of the Jack Campbell branch, working on his uncle and aunt’s farm — the Gilbert and Mary Lynch farm in the district) to the Keeseekoowenin cemetery. He showed Glen the graves of Moses Burns (Chief Keeseekoowenin) and Harriet Burns Campbell, and told him: ‘because they were Indians.’ This was the first disclosure of Indigenous ancestry to the Jack Campbell branch. \[A — The White Feather, 2024\]

The Moses Burns gravestone reads: ‘Moses Burns. Died at Elphinstone Apr 10, 1906\. Aged 87 yrs. Chief of Okanase Indian Reserve at Riding Mountain.’ Photographed in The White Feather. The gravestone confirms: death date April 10, 1906 \[consistent with project record of April 1906\]; age 87 gives birth year \~1819 \[consistent with project estimate of c.1818\]; formal title as Chief of Okanase Indian Reserve at Riding Mountain. \[A — gravestone photograph\]

\#\# \*\*8.4 Significance for the Project\*\*

Tom Knott’s role as disclosure vector is a structural datum for the project. He is the grandson of Alfred William Knott (the research anchor) and Nora Campbell Knott (Glen’s daughter and Harriet’s daughter). He held the knowledge of the family’s Indigenous ancestry across the suppression era and chose to disclose it to a member of the Jack Campbell branch — the branch where it had been most thoroughly suppressed. The disclosure mechanism was direct, personal, and geographic: standing at the graves.

Tom Knott thus connects two research lines: the Knott family thread (as Alfred and Nora’s grandson) and the Campbell/Burns/Keeseekoowenin thread (as the person who broke the suppression and connected the Jack Campbell branch to its Indigenous ancestry). \[A\]

\#\# \*\*8.5 Stacey Knott Coordination Note\*\*

Tom Knott’s CN Rail service plaque sits on Stacey Knott’s desk. She was close to him and knew he carried family knowledge. As of June 8, 2026, Stacey has not yet read The White Feather — she has expressed interest in reading it (Messenger, June 8, 2026). Jon intends to present it to her that evening. Staged disclosure protocol remains in effect.

Information management note: Stacey’s engagement with The White Feather as of June 8, 2026 is logged. Staged disclosure protocol remains in effect for broader findings. Her reading of the book is self-directed and represents her own process.

\#\# \*\*8.6 Tom Knott as Family and Local Historian — Upgraded \[V57\]\*\*

The thin observation-tier note logged in V56 is superseded. Three independent sources now converge on the same character:

\- \*\*Glen III’s correspondence \[A\]:\*\* “Tom Knott was a great guy… He was looking after the Gilbert Plains museum at the time, and we shared a passion for history.”

\- \*\*Stacey’s recollection \[B\]:\*\* Tom loved history, kept generally to himself, repeatedly discussed the family’s Indigenous ancestry, and was often ignored by extended family when he raised it; he also regularly brought unusual (“exotic”) drinks when visiting — a personal-character detail logged for continuity, not evidentiary weight.

\- \*\*\*The White Feather\*\*\*\*\* \*\*\*\*\[A\]:\*\* documents Tom actively guiding Glen III through historical locations, taking him to the Burns graves, explaining Keeseekoowenin ancestry, and connecting landscape to family history (Section 8.3).

Tom Knott should no longer be read as merely “interested in history.” The convergent evidence supports a consistent role: active local historian, Gilbert Plains Museum caretaker, family-history transmitter, and someone who intentionally worked to ensure younger generations understood their ancestry — set against an extended family environment that largely did not want to hear it. \[B — convergent, with the museum-caretaker detail itself at A via direct correspondence\]

\#\# \*\*8.7 Aunt Mary — Identity Confirmed and Second-Generation Suppression \[V57\]\*\*

“Aunt Mary,” referenced in family correspondence, is identified as Mary B. Campbell (b. 1919), daughter of Jack Campbell and Dorothy Turner, and Glen III’s direct aunt. This converges with two existing framework references: “Mary Campbell Lynch,” already cited at \[B\] in Section 7.3 as the 1998 interview source for Jack Campbell’s sons’ WWII deaths, and “the Gilbert and Mary Lynch farm” named in Section 8.3 as the farm where a young Glen Campbell was working at the time of the 1968 cemetery visit. The convergence of all three references onto one person is logged at \[B\] — a well-supported identity inference, though the Campbell-to-Lynch married-name link is not independently documented.

Mary’s testimony, describing her own upbringing rather than repeating family folklore, establishes a second-generation repetition of the identity-suppression instruction already on record for Glen (Section 7.2): her father, Jack Campbell, warned his own children never to admit they had “Indian blood.” Mary concealed her ancestry for much of her life, later regretted it, and told Glen III: “Don’t let other people’s ignorance do the same to you.” This is logged as distinct from, and reinforcing, the existing Glen-level suppression finding — showing the pattern was actively repeated across two generations rather than passively inherited. \[B — named oral source, consistent with the established interview pattern\]

\*\*\[V58 ADDITION\] Expanded Aunt Mary testimony — sibling-differentiated responses and specific incidents \[A — The White Feather, Glen Campbell, 2024\]:\*\*

\*Sibling differential:\* Uncle Fred (Fred Campbell, F/Sgt Frederick Campbell, 1920–1943, WWII airman) did not follow the silence and was subjected to racist taunts at school as a result. First documented instance of the suppression failing for one sibling while holding for another within the same household.

\*Family vocabulary \[A\]:\* The Keeseekoowenin Reserve was referred to within the family as “Keesee.” Reserve relatives were called “the Aunties.” Confirmed in-family terms.

\*Prohibition and residential school knowledge:\* Mary was forbidden by her father from visiting the reserve. Her cousins who were permitted relayed stories about children taken from families and sent to schools where they suffered abuse. The prohibition and the content of the cousins’ stories are \[A\]; Mary’s retrospective rationale for the prohibition is \[B\].

\*Curling banquet avoidance incident \[A\]:\* Mary deliberately avoided a reserve cousin at a curling banquet, fearing someone would ask how she knew him. Specific, datable-in-principle adult incident; illustrates the suppression maintained actively into adulthood, not only received passively in childhood.

\*Irish ancestry cover story \[B — open thread\]:\* Glen III’s pre-1968 understanding included a believed Irish family line on his mother’s side; no Irish ancestry was subsequently located in the tree. Logged as a possible third cover story — distinct from the French-ancestry cover (dark complexion) and the Scottish-ancestry emphasis (Glen Sr.). Whether it is a separate branch of the suppression, informal family lore, or an unrelated genealogical error is not yet determined. Open thread, no action assigned.

\#\# \*\*8.8 Stacey Knott Coordination Note — Continuation \[V57\]\*\*

Two further items, logged for continuity under the existing staged-disclosure framing (Section 8.5):

\- Stacey independently recalls Tom repeatedly telling her she was “1/18th Native.” The significance is the transmission itself — Tom passing knowledge of the Burns/Keeseekoowenin ancestry to a younger generation — not the numerical fraction. \[B — oral tradition\]

\- Stacey has shown increasing ease discussing documented family history (sharing First Nations creators, volunteering memories of Tom, a comfortable exchange about bannock). Consistent with the existing framing, this is read as growing comfort with already-documented family history, not a change in identity or any claim to legal status. Her engagement remains self-directed; the staged-disclosure protocol remains in effect.

\*\*\[V61 ADDITION\] Manuscript milestone —\*\*\*\* \*\*\*\*\*The Campbell–Knott Family\*\*\*\*\*, Edition 1.0 \[B — direct observation\]:\*\* A printed copy of the manuscript was reviewed by Stacey prior to distribution to the wider family. Her engagement — initial deflective humour (“That’s not romantic”), followed by voluntary sustained reading, photograph examination, direct questions about specific ancestors, and a stated emotional response (“I have tears in my eyes”) — represents a significant point of progress within the staged-disclosure framing above: the first time the documented family history was received by her as a complete, chronological narrative with supporting photographs, rather than through the piecemeal prior disclosure recorded elsewhere in this section and in Section 8.3. She requested to share the manuscript with her father, indicating she regards it as belonging to the family rather than solely as a research project. A legitimate editorial correction was independently identified by Stacey during this review: manuscript wording (“Their sons were T.S. (Tom) Knott… and Daniel Cameron Knott…”) implies John Robert Cameron Knott and Margaret Joyce Spencer had only two children, when only the direct ancestral line is being traced. Logged as a manuscript-track correction for Edition 1.1 — either list all known siblings or add an explicit “direct line only” note. This item is separate from the evidentiary framework and does not affect any evidence-tier finding. \# \*\*9. COMMUNITY FORMATION\*\*

\#\# \*\*9.2 Duncan Cameron — Corridor’s Commercial Node\*\*

Duncan Cameron (born Oban, Scotland) homesteaded NW-22-24-22W1 in spring 1889 — the second European settler after Glen Campbell. He came to Elphinstone and was employed by the late Robert Campbell before establishing his own operation. The nearest railway points were Strathclair and Neepawa; the last 100 miles of his initial journey were completed on foot. \[A — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\] The commercial nucleus of the early settlement — first general store (1890, initially at Campbell farm, then moved to Cameron’s homestead where it was roofed with shingles — the first shingled roof in the district), Glenlyon Post Office (1891, Cameron as postmaster), Gilbert Plains School (1891, Cameron donated the land), Beef Ring building (NE-22-24-22W1, 1923–1951; Municipal Heritage Site No. 280, designated August 8, 2006 — last surviving Beef Ring building in Manitoba) — all concentrated at or immediately adjacent to this section. \[A — multiple sources; A — MHS Gilbert Plains Sites Report, 02 June 2026\] \*\*\[V54\]\*\* Memory Lane identifies the original Beef Ring membership list, which includes A. William Knott alongside Campbell, Rowat, Hamilton, Yuill, Lee, and others. This is the first named institutional membership confirmation for Alfred Knott in the district, placing him as a founding member of a cooperative economic institution centred on the same section as Cameron’s commercial nucleus. \[A — Memory Lane, via Gilbert Plains Manitoba History Facebook group\] \*\*\[V43\] Renicker Creek\*\* is confirmed as a named watercourse running through Section 22 T24 R22W (Cameron’s parcel). \[A — MHS Gilbert Plains Sites Report, 02 June 2026\] Cameron was a Member of the United Church and the Masonic Order, a Massey-Harris agent, the first Reeve of the Rural Municipality, Mayor several years, and MLA 1907–1910. He operated the Cameron Department Store until his death in 1942\. \[V63 ADDITION\] A provincial bye-election was held in Gilbert Plains 17 November 1908 to fill the vacancy caused by Glen Campbell’s election to the House of Commons; Cameron won (894 votes) over A.D. Cummings (855), a majority of 39 \[A — Free Press Prairie Farmer, 15 Jul 1914, reporting retrospectively on 1908\]. This adds a precise date and result to Cameron’s civic career, situating his provincial-level service between his 1901 municipal Reeve election and the 1904 close of his first Reeve term. \*\*\[V53\]\*\* His 1901 election as first Reeve was proposed by Thomas Embleton and James H. Crowe (see Section 9.5) \[A — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\]. The McTavish connection: John McTavish appears in the 1889 settler list alongside Cameron’s arrival; Cameron married Christina McTavish in 1896, consistent with a founding-wave family network. \[A — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\]

\*\*\[V55 ADDITION\] Gilbert Plains Flour Mill \[A\]:\*\* A company was formed in 1904 by Duncan Cameron, Glen A. Campbell, and Joshua Law to establish a flour mill and elevator at Gilbert Plains. The mill was sold in 1906 to A.W. Heath. \[A — photograph and caption, Gilbert Plains Manitoba History Facebook group\] This adds a third economic-development register — alongside the general store and Beef Ring already documented above — in which Cameron and Glen Campbell appear as joint commercial actors: settlement → agriculture → processing → commercial development, all anchored to the same Section 22 nucleus. \*\*\[V64\]\*\* Independent newspaper corroboration: Winnipeg Tribune, 25 Jul 1904, p.10, confirms the company formation and supplies the formal corporate name — “The Gilbert Plains Milling Co., Ltd.” — and the financing mechanism: the mill was built under a $3,000 municipal bonus, with the money-raising by-law put to a vote 30 July 1904, in the municipality Cameron had led as first Reeve. The mill was thus a publicly subsidized venture, tying the commercial nucleus into the civic-formation layer. Location given as “Gilbert Plains village,” a useful mid-1904 designation datum for the incorporation sequence \[A\].

\#\# \*\*9.3 Glenlyon and Gilbert Plains — Two Distinct Settlements\*\*

The early settlement nucleus — Ross cabin, Scott homestead, Campbell ranch, Cameron store — was known as Glenlyon. Modern Gilbert Plains is a different settlement, formed around the railway junction in T25 R22W1, north of the original Glenlyon nucleus. All directional references in later narrative sources use the modern townsite as their reference point. \[A — settlement geography report\] \*\*\[V53\]\*\* This finding receives independent corroboration from outside the framework’s own source base: in correspondence (1 June 2026), Dr. Gordon Goldsborough, Acting Executive Director of the Manitoba Historical Society, referred unprompted to “the community of Glenlyon” as a place associated with the RM of Gilbert Plains, consistent with Cameron’s 1891 Glenlyon Post Office (Section 9.2) and with this section’s pre-existing finding. Goldsborough is also the author of the MHS Memorable Manitobans entry for Glenlyon Campbell already load-bearing for the Thomas MacDonald Johnstone thread (Section 6.4.2), making this a second, independent point of contact between his published work and the framework. \[A — Goldsborough correspondence, 1 June 2026\]

\#\# \*\*9.4 Walter Archibald Scott (\*\*\*\*‘\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*Baldy\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*’\*\*\*\*) — Biographical Record\*\*

Born 8 October 1864, Sunderland, Durham. Family home: Creiff, Perthshire. Educated at Creiff school and Glasgow University. Came to Canada around 1884 to Fort Ellice as a clerk. Joined Bolton’s Scouts, Riel Rebellion 1885 — met Glen Campbell, returned with him to Merchiston Ranch. Married Victoria Burns, youngest daughter of Chief Keeseekoowenin, 1886\. Confirmed in the 1889 settler list; homestead Sections 4/5 T24 R22W1 (Military Homestead No. 2639, 24 December 1891). \[V63 ADDITION\] The Campbell–Scott partnership formed in 1885 is independently confirmed in the press as early as 27 August 1889, when Scott and Glen Campbell are jointly described as cattle ranchers at Big Grass \[A — Manitoba Free Press, 27 Aug 1889 (daily); reprinted Manitoba Weekly Free Press, 29 Aug 1889\]; livestock inventory updated 2 October 1890 (300 sheep, 100 cattle, 100+ horses) \[A — Manitoba Weekly Free Press, 2 Oct 1890\]. Sold homestead for Klondike expedition 1898 — precisely dated: Glen Campbell organized the expedition for early February 1898, travelling via Edmonton and Peace River, with Scott named explicitly as his partner, securing dogs and arrangements \[A — Free Press Prairie Farmer, 16 Dec 1897, reprinted from the Minnedosa Tribune\]; returned broke \~1900. Managed Riding Mountain Ranch for Lord Vivian 1900–1916. Served 107th Battalion WWI; returned from England late 1917 on age and medical discharge. Moved west to Alberta, BC, then California. Worked in Hollywood stables associated with Mary Pickford and Douglas Fairbanks. Later managed stables in the Los Angeles area. Died in California caring for and training horses. \[A — Our Story to 1970; A — Our Story to 1984; A — 1889 settler list, Settlers of the Plains, 1953\]

\#\# \*\*9.5 James H. Crowe — 1889 Founding Settler and Horse Breeder\*\*

Born 1865, Aaron, Bruce County, Ontario. Came to Manitoba 1885 with the volunteer Army Corps for the Riel Rebellion — the same mobilisation that brought Glen Campbell and Walter Scott together. Returned to Ontario 1886\. Came to Gilbert Plains 1889 via Strathclair, completing the journey on foot as part of the founding settler wave. Held land at Sec 20, T24 R22W1 \[A — 1894 mail petition, RG 3 B4 Vol. 65 File 359/1894; see Section 9.6\]. \[A — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\]

Crowe became celebrated for his Percheron horse breeding operation. His competitive record: 1922 Toronto Royal — Grand Royal Seal for Champion Percheron; 1924 Chicago International Fair — Reserve Grand Championship and Canadian Grand Championship. Life Member, Manitoba Percheron Horse Club, 1947\. Died 23 March 1949\. \[A — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\]

Corridor significance: Crowe’s Percheron operation alongside the Riding Mountain Ranch’s Clydesdales, Hackneys, and Thoroughbreds confirms the Gilbert Plains corridor as a serious horse-breeding district from the founding period. The horse economy of the corridor was not incidental to settlement — it was one of its primary productive functions. \[B — geographic and economic interpretation\] \*\*\[V53\]\*\* Crowe also co-proposed Duncan Cameron’s 1901 election as the Rural Municipality’s first Reeve, alongside Thomas Embleton (see Section 9.2), placing both founding horse-breeders within the same early municipal leadership circle that would later select Embleton as a Canadian Farmer Delegate. \[A — Settlers of the Plains, 1953\]

\*\*\[V53 addition\] Early contemporary confirmation of the horse economy \[A\]:\*\* The Manitoba Daily Free Press, 6 May 1890, reports that Glenlyon Campbell and others would be sending “fancy horses” to the district’s upcoming 24 May celebrations — a contemporary sighting of Campbell in community life only a few years after his 1884 arrival, and the earliest dated confirmation in the framework of the horse economy’s social as well as productive role in the corridor. \[A — Manitoba Daily Free Press, 6 May 1890, via Gilbert Plains Manitoba History Facebook group\]

\#\# \*\*9.6 Gilbert Plains Mail Petition, January 1894 — Community Scale and Carbert Placement \[V38 ADDITION\]\*\*

RG 3, B4, Volume 65, File 359/1894 (Reel T-2185) contains a petition dated January 1894 addressed to W.W. McLeod, Post Office Inspector, Winnipeg, signed by residents of Gilbert Plains, Marquette, Manitoba. The petition text states that mail facilities are inadequate for the settlement and requests weekly mail service. The Post Office Inspector’s response letter (No. 359, dated 2 February 1894\) confirms the settlement’s scale and proposes a new route.

\*\*Community scale, 1894 \[A\]:\*\* The Inspector’s letter records that Gilbert Plains served approximately 50 families at the time of the petition, with annual postal revenue of approximately $80.00. The petition itself carries well over 100 individual signatures across four continuation sheets, consistent with this household count.

\*\*Carbert family at Section 21, T24 R22W1 \[A\]:\*\* The petition signatories include George Carbert, George Carbert Jr., and Elizabeth Carbert, all recorded with DLS coordinates Sec 21, Twp 24, Rge 22\. This is direct primary source confirmation that the Carbert family occupied Section 21 T24 R22W1 as of January 1894 — seventeen years before Alfred Knott’s 1911 placement in the Carbert household at the same section. Joseph Carbert appears separately at Sec 3, Twp 25, Rge 22 (one township north), indicating the family held land across the district.

\*\*Known network families confirmed \[A\]:\*\* The petition includes John Scott (Sec 14, T24 R22W1), James McCurdy, the Morris family cluster (W.R. Morris, M. Morris, E. Morris, Frank A. Morris — Sec 9–10 T24 R22W1), the Doak family (William Doak, W.H. Doak Jr., Mrs. Wm. Doak, Miss Lizzie Doak — Sec 13 T24 R22W1), James D. and Mrs. Sutherland (Sec 18, T24 R21W1), and James H. Crowe (Sec 20, T24 R22W1 — see Section 9.5). The Rowlett family cluster and Eastment family appear on the continuation sheets.

\*\*Mail corridor geography \[A\]:\*\* The Post Office Inspector’s proposed route runs Gilbert Plains → Oaknook → Rigby → Lake Dauphin, 29 miles, weekly. This route maps directly onto the prairie-edge escarpment corridor documented in Section 4\. The existing fortnightly route (Gilbert Plains → Lake Dauphin direct, 26 miles) confirms that Lake Dauphin was the established hub connection. A Post Office at Oaknook had recently been authorized to be supplied fortnightly from Gilbert Plains, placing Oaknook as a secondary node on the same corridor. \[A — consistent with escarpment corridor model, Section 3\]

\*\*Significance for placement mechanism:\*\* The Carbert family’s confirmed presence at Section 21 T24 R22W1 in 1894 — and their documented community standing as petition signatories — establishes that the Carbert farm was a settled, named household within the community for at least seventeen years before Alfred’s arrival. This strengthens the \[B\] inference that Alfred’s placement at the Carbert farm was pre-arranged through community knowledge of specific receiving households, not an ad hoc assignment. \[B\]

\*\*Primary source:\*\* RG 3, B4, Volume 65, File 359/1894. Reel T-2185, images 793–799. Post Office Department, Manitoba. Petition dated January 1894; Inspector’s letter No. 359, dated 2 February 1894\.

\#\# \*\*9.7 J.L. Parker —\*\*\*\* \*\*\*\*\*Gilbert Plains History Highlights\*\*\*\*\* \*\*\*\*(1981) \[V54 ADDITION\]\*\*

\#\#\# Source Identification

\*\*Title:\*\* \*Gilbert Plains History Highlights\* | \*\*Compiler:\*\* J.L. Parker | \*\*Published:\*\* 75th Anniversary Committee, 1981 | \*\*Origin:\*\* Originally serialized in the \*Gilbert Plains Maple Leaf\*; described as a supplement to \*Settlers of the Plains\* (1953).

Parker’s compilation draws on the 1890 DLS survey transcriptions and community records, making it an independently compiled local-history source whose findings can be tested against primary DLS and census evidence.

\#\#\# Harriet Campbell on the 1890 Survey — Independent Corroboration \[A\]

Parker’s transcription of the 1890 surveyor’s township map includes the notation: \*\*“\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*Harriet Campbell — barn and field.\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*”\*\*

This independently confirms physical improvements on Harriet Campbell’s land at the time of the 1890 DLS survey. It corroborates the established Burns/Campbell occupation landscape and adds a specific detail: a barn and field were present at survey time. \[A — Parker 1981, 1890 survey transcription\]

Framework significance: the barn-and-field notation places Harriet in active agricultural occupation at the corridor exit point before the formal scrip grant (Crown Grant 1895). This is consistent with all prior evidence and adds physical infrastructure detail to the occupation record.

\#\#\# Municipal Founding Leadership \[A\]

Parker confirms the founding Rural Municipality leadership network: Thomas Embleton; James Crowe; Duncan Cameron; Robert Greenaway; James Childs; and others.

This strengthens the interpretation that the leadership network later involved in immigration promotion — Embleton as Farmer Delegate, Cameron as first Reeve, Crowe as co-proposer of Cameron’s election — was constituted as a recognizable community cohort at municipal formation, not assembled ad hoc for immigration activities. \[A — Parker 1981\]

\#\#\# School and Church Records \[A\]

Parker preserves: Gilbert Plains School No. 630 formation; trustee lists; early pupil lists; Methodist congregation membership lists; Chatsworth committee membership. The pupil lists offer potential for identifying settler family children not in census or land records; the Chatsworth committee membership may cross-reference with the Chatsworth Cemetery burial record (Sec 34 T24 R22W — Knott family confirmed, MHS). \[A — Parker 1981\]

\#\#\# Newspaper Corroboration \[A\]

Two contemporary newspaper articles confirm the 1890–1891 occupation landscape:

\*\*August 1890:\*\* Four DLS parties active in the Lake Dauphin district; one specifically surveying a road from the M.\&N.W. to “the Gilbert Plains.” Uses “the Gilbert Plains” as a recognized geographic designation before the modern townsite existed. \[A\]

\*\*January 1891:\*\* O.C. Blake letter reports Arthur Willey growing White Fife wheat at T24 R22 Section 28\. Matches Parker’s survey transcription placing Willey on the same section. Earliest dated primary-source confirmation of a named settler producing a documented crop at a confirmed DLS location. \[A\]

\[Source: August 1890 newspaper; January 1891 newspaper (O.C. Blake letter) — both via Gilbert Plains Manitoba History Facebook group\]

\#\# \*\*9.8 District-Before-Town — Settlement Sequence Formalized \[V54 ADDITION\]\*\*

The Parker material, newspaper corroboration, and existing DLS/census evidence collectively support formalizing a settlement sequence finding that has been implicit in the framework since V18 but not previously stated as a discrete named finding.

\*\*The Gilbert Plains district existed as a functioning settlement landscape before the town of Gilbert Plains existed.\*\*

The sequence:

| \*\*Stage\*\* | \*\*Content\*\* | \*\*Approximate Period\*\* |  
| \--- | \--- | \--- |  
| 1\. Geographic corridor | Escarpment exit at Sec 1 T24 R22W1 | Pre-settlement |  
| 2\. Wagiiwing homeland | Indigenous occupation, trail network, named landscape | Pre-1840 |  
| 3\. Métis/HBC stopping-place | Ross cabin; Burns/Scott/Campbell families | 1870s–1884 |  
| 4\. Agricultural settler arrival | Campbell 1884; 1889 settler wave; DLS surveys with named occupants | 1884–1890 |  
| 5\. Trail-connected family network | School 1891; mail petition 1894; Beef Ring 1890s | 1891–1900 |  
| 6\. Civic formation | RM established ∼1901; Cameron as first Reeve; Masonic Lodge 1905 | 1901–1905 |  
| 7\. Commercial concentration | General store at Cameron’s section; Glenlyon Post Office | 1890–1905 |  
| 8\. Railway arrival | Townsite in T25 R22W1 becomes regional hub | ∼1905–1912 |  
| 9\. Modern Gilbert Plains | Railway-era service centre over a settlement landscape two decades old | 1905–present |

All later narrative sources describe the district from the modern townsite as their reference point. The DLS, Parker, and newspaper evidence establishes the reverse sequence: the district was the founding unit; the town was a later consolidation. \[B — framework interpretation; A — individual supporting items throughout this section\]

This finding is directly load-bearing for the planned settlement formation report for MHS and local historical societies.

\# \*\*10. RAILWAY REORGANISATION AND MODERN GILBERT PLAINS\*\*

The railway shifted the commercial and administrative centre of gravity northward from the original Glenlyon nucleus to the townsite in T25 R22W1. The founding families retained their land at the original locations while new commercial infrastructure grew around the railway station to the north. By approximately 1912, when Glen Campbell sold his farm to Joe Berry and relocated to Winnipeg, the transition was effectively complete.

The railway itself followed the prairie edge of the escarpment corridor. Modern Gilbert Plains at T25 R22W1 is a late expression of the same geographic forcing that determined every earlier settlement in the corridor. \[B — geographic interpretation\]

Riding Mountain Ranch disposal and community network closure: When the Riding Mountain Ranch was sold at the outbreak of WWI, Frank Hyde of Elphinstone was among the purchasers. Frank Hyde was the husband of Agnes Johnstone — daughter of John Charles Johnstone, grandson of the Atkinson/Cardinal marriage at Kennis Creek (see Section 5.9). This connects the Kennis Creek community network directly to the final disposal of the ranch that had employed Walter Scott, hosted Lord Vivian, and grown from Robert Campbell’s original Okanase Band summer camping ground. \[A/B — Our Story to 1970\]

WWI casualties from the reserve network: A. Flett and S. Flett — sons of George Flett Burns (eldest son of Chief Keeseekoowenin, see Section 5.4) — were killed in action. Their names are inscribed on the Cenotaph in Strathclair. Quite a number of the Keeseekoowenin Reserve Band joined the 107th Regiment under Lt. Col. Glen Campbell. The reserve’s participation in the 107th is a direct expression of the long-standing Campbell/Burns relationship documented throughout this framework. \[A — RM Strathclair history\]

\# \*\*11. KNOTT INTEGRATION AND CONTINUITY\*\*

\*\*\[V43\] Interpretive note — Alfred Edmund 1909 visit:\*\* The father’s tourist visit to Oxbow, Saskatchewan in September 1909 substantially strengthens the interpretation that Alfred William’s 1911 migration was a family-managed decision rather than an isolated act by a 17-year-old. The visit is \[A\] confirmed. Whether Alfred Edmund was visiting William Thomas Knott (whose 1908–1910 movements between Calgary arrival and Gilbert Plains destination remain partially unaccounted for) is a plausible working hypothesis \[C\]. Whether Alfred Edmund actively arranged Alfred William’s migration is a further inference \[C\] that must not be promoted from the confirmed visit alone. GPT observation (June 2026): ‘The father’s 1909 visit significantly strengthens the broader idea that Alfred William’s migration was a family-managed decision, not an isolated act by a 17-year-old.’ Under the five-layer infrastructure model, family-level coordination and the formal British Bonus mechanism are not mutually exclusive — the family decision is the context; the infrastructure is the channel through which it was executed. \[B\]

\#\# \*\*11.2 Placement Mechanism — Ranked Hypotheses\*\*

\[B\] \#1 Uncle Relay: William Thomas Knott arrived Canada May 1, 1908 (SS Empress of Britain, departed Liverpool; destination recorded as Calgary, Alta.) \[A — BT27 / LAC manifest\]. Alfred departed March 1911 (SS Tunisian) — three years later, not one as previously estimated. Relay structure documented; local contacts not yet located.

William Thomas Knott — March 1910 Arrival and Destination Analysis: A March 1910 arrival record identifies William Thomas Knott as a returning Canadian. The destination field of this manifest is partially obscured by a large “RET’D CANADIANS” stamp affecting handwriting legibility. Analysis methodology: the original manifest page was examined directly; multiple image enhancement methods were applied including inversion and contrast adjustment; additional stamp impressions from the same manifest series were located and used to reconstruct a clean stamp profile; handwriting comparison was conducted against nearby destination entries on the same page. Findings: \[A\] the destination province field appears to read Manitoba; \[A\] the destination entry appears to begin with a capital G; \[A\] a second capital letter consistent with P is visible beneath the stamp; \[A\] stamp-subtraction analysis produced a partial reconstruction consistent with “Plains” as the second word. Independent observer test: to reduce confirmation bias, a cropped image containing only the obscured destination field was shown to an independent observer (Stacey Knott) without disclosure of the research question, without identified destination candidates, and without surrounding columns or the province field visible. The observer’s immediate unprompted response was “Gilbert Plains.” \[A — documented observer response\] The convergence of handwriting analysis, stamp reconstruction, province field reading, and blind observer identification is \[B\] strongly consistent with Gilbert Plains, Manitoba as the destination recorded for William Thomas Knott on the March 1910 manifest. The stamp interference prevents absolute confirmation from the surviving microfilm image; Gilbert Plains is the current leading interpretation. \[C — destination reading pending primary source confirmation\]

Migration chronology revised \[V43 updated\]: 1908 — William Thomas Knott enters Western Canada, destination Calgary \[A\]; September 1909 — Alfred Edmund Knott (Alfred William’s father) arrives Quebec as tourist, final destination Oxbow, Saskatchewan \[A\]; March 1910 — William Thomas Knott returns to Canada as a returning Canadian, destination appearing to record Gilbert Plains, Manitoba \[B\]; March 1911 — Alfred William Knott arrives under British Bonus, destination appearing to record Gilbert Plains, Manitoba \[B\]. \[B\] Under this sequence, both William Thomas Knott (1910) and Alfred William Knott (1911) appear anchored to Gilbert Plains at the point of arrival. The district was not reached after dispersal from a hub centre such as Winnipeg or Brandon — it appears as the recorded destination in both cases. \[B\] This strengthens the uncle relay model materially: rather than Alfred independently finding his way to Gilbert Plains through an unknown placement process, the evidence is consistent with the district being a known target within the family migration network before Alfred boarded the SS Tunisian in March 1911\.

Alfred’s manifest — direct destination significance: many immigrants arriving in Western Canada in this period listed major distribution centres (Winnipeg, Brandon, Calgary, Regina) as destination, with final settlement reached later. Alfred’s manifest appears to name Gilbert Plains directly — a small rural settlement district in western Manitoba — rather than a hub city. \[B\] This suggests Alfred was travelling to a destination already identified before departure, not entering Canada for later placement and dispersal.

1906 arrival discrepancy (working note, June 2026): The 1911 Canadian census records Alfred’s immigration year as 1906, not 1911\. These two sources are in direct contradiction. The SS Tunisian March 1911 manifest is \[A\]; the 1911 census immigration year is \[A\] as recorded, \[B\] as accurate date (self-reported, common source of error). Three interpretations: (1) census year is a transcription or memory error and 1911 is correct; (2) Alfred arrived 1906 via a route not yet searched, returned to England, then departed again 1911 on the SS Tunisian; (3) the 1908 gasfitter record (location not yet confirmed) may clarify whether he was in England or Canada in 1908\. Search has been expanded to include US land border crossing routes (Minnesota/North Dakota entry to Manitoba) — prior search covered ocean arrivals only. To investigate next session: confirm 1908 gasfitter record location; search US land border manifests 1905–1907; evaluate whether a 1906 arrival followed by return fits the family transatlantic movement pattern (Beatrice, William Thomas, Alfred all making multiple crossings). \[Open flag — do not revise placement mechanism timeline until resolved\]

\[B\] \#2 Masonic Network: Alfred Edmund Knott (Alfred’s father) initiated Cherwell Lodge No. 599, Banbury, 1903\. Glen Campbell was Director of Ceremonies, Robbie Burns Lodge No. 103, Gilbert Plains, instituted 9 March 1905\. Lodge-to-lodge link not yet directly documented. Under the layered infrastructure model (see Section 11.5), the Masonic network is a possible contact mechanism operating within the larger placement system, not an alternative explanation to it.

\[C\] \#3 PSEL / British Bonus Institutional Channel: ‘British Bonus Allowed’ on Alfred’s manifest confirms he entered under an agricultural placement framework. Alfred’s school and HMC affiliation unidentified. Under the layered infrastructure model (see Section 11.5), the British Bonus scheme is a government processing mechanism within the larger system, not a placement origin in itself.

\*\*Framework revision note (V37):\*\* The previous framing treated the uncle relay, Masonic network, and British Bonus as competing alternative explanations for Alfred’s arrival. The V37 immigration infrastructure findings (Section 11.5) establish that these mechanisms were not alternatives — they were potentially simultaneous layers within a mature, integrated recruitment and placement system operating between Britain and western Canada by 1907–1911. The framework question has been revised accordingly: not ‘which mechanism brought Alfred?’ but ‘at which level of the system did Alfred’s specific connection occur?’

RG76 placement mechanism research (June 2026): RG76-I-A-1, Vol. 569, File 813390 examined. File covers C.E. Baring Young, of Daylesford, Chipping Norton, Oxfordshire — a small private training school sending 6–8 boys annually to a receiving farm at Woodstock, Ontario (manager: H.G. Benfield, Havelock Farm). Operation documented via correspondence with W.D. Scott, Superintendent of Immigration, Ottawa, 1909–1940. Key operational details confirmed \[A\]: boys batched annually; London office issued landing letters in February for March sailings (same calendar pattern as Alfred’s March 1911 arrival); placement not restricted to farming — boys also placed into carpentry, blacksmithing, and kindred trades via Young’s Canadian agent; Woodstock farm served as fallback base. By July 1910 Baring Young was requesting permission to scale to 10–20 boys per year. SS Tunisian confirmed \[A\] as a vessel used by this placement stream (W. Briers, February 1912 landing letter, same file). Baring Young is not Alfred’s placement channel — all documented placements are Woodstock, Ontario, not Manitoba \[A\]. File retained as framework evidence: provides primary source documentation of the February-letter/March-sailing mechanism and British Bonus landing letter format operative in Alfred’s arrival year.

Search pivot (June 2026): UK-end search for Oxfordshire/Warwickshire sending organizations set aside in favour of Canadian-end approach. Next target: identify receiving farms in the Gilbert Plains / Dauphin district registered with the Immigration Branch or corresponding with the Dauphin immigration agent, 1909–1912. Carbert farm (NE-21-024-22-W1) is the anchor. Whether Glen Campbell was registered as a British Bonus receiving host remains open — a Campbell registration in RG76 Dauphin district files would significantly strengthen the placement mechanism analysis. \[C — pending search\]

Carbert placement refinement: Alfred was placed with the Carbert household rather than with Glen Campbell directly — likely because Campbell was absent from the district on parliamentary duties (1908–1912). \[B\]

CPR Land Sales context: The Carbert farm (NE-21-024-22-W1) was itself a CPR land purchase — George Carbert, July 9 1895, 160 acres, $3/acre, paid in full. \[A — CPR Land Sales, SANDS, Contract 7296, Volume 112\] Alfred’s 1911 placement on CPR-purchased land, arriving via CPR infrastructure (SS Tunisian, British Bonus Allowed), is consistent with a CPR-integrated placement mechanism.

Knott family Canadian emigration pattern: The family’s Canadian network was active and bilateral across the 1910s. Beatrice Jessie Knott (1895–1990), Alfred’s sister, emigrated to Canada arriving 15 May 1914; she moved through Quebec and Revelstoke BC before settling in Seattle WA, and returned to Fenny Compton, Warwickshire in 1920\. \[A — passenger records\] Alfred and Beatrice were therefore both in Canada simultaneously 1914–1920. Alfred’s own return visit: his 1921 passenger declaration records his purpose as ‘for a holiday’ — Beatrice was almost certainly present at Fenny Compton at the same time, as both returned in the 1920–21 period. \[A — passenger records; B — inference from contemporaneous dates\] A half-sister, Anne Elizabeth Knott (1932–2019), was born in Islington, London, in 1932 to Josephine Cockbaine; she died in La Crescent, Minnesota, USA. \[A — vital records\]

\*\*\[V55 working note\]\*\*\*\* \*\*\*\*“\*\*\*\*Farm Pupils\*\*\*\*”\*\*\*\* \*\*\*\*terminology (RG17 Vol. 696 File 79744, August 1891):\*\* A government file using this exact term has been located in the Minnedosa-area record (Section 11.5.16). The file predates Alfred’s 1911 arrival by twenty years and is not evidence of his specific placement mechanism; it confirms only that the terminology and an associated administrative/supervisory apparatus existed in the district well before his arrival. Logged as a \[C\] terminology lead, not a mechanism finding. Direct examination of file contents is the next step.

\#\# \*\*11.3 Marriage into the Campbell Line\*\*

Alfred Knott married Elleonora (Nora) Campbell on 20 November 1912\. Nora was Glen Campbell’s daughter, born 11 April 1891 at Elphinstone \[A — RG 10\], Harriet Burns’s daughter, Keeseekoowenin’s granddaughter. Alfred was placed in the community in March 1911 and married the host’s daughter twenty months later. Dunae: ‘they married their tutors’ daughters.’ \[A — Dunae, Cannington Manor\]

First newspaper confirmation: The Dauphin Herald death notice (October 22, 1917\) names Glen’s surviving children as: Mrs. Christina Waddy; Mary Campbell of Winnipeg; Mrs. Elleonora Knott of Gilbert Plains; and Private Jack Campbell. This is the first contemporary primary-source document directly naming Elleonora Nora Campbell as Mrs. Knott of Gilbert Plains. \[A\] The Knott placement in Gilbert Plains is now anchored to the Campbell family connection at \[A\] level.

\*\*\[V56 ADDITION\] Alfred William Knott Masonic grave marker \[A\]:\*\* A photographed gravestone confirms Alfred William Knott (1893–1977) and Eleanora “Nora” Knott (1891–1975) buried together under “Knott,” with a Masonic emblem (square and compass, “G”) on Alfred William’s own portion of the marker. Both dates match the existing record exactly. This corrects a GPT-sourced detail that had attributed a grave marker/Masonic emblem to William Thomas Knott (Alfred’s uncle) — William Thomas died in Oxfordshire in 1960 and has no Manitoba grave, so that attribution is retired as a misidentification, consistent with the documented pattern of GPT confirming image content it had not actually examined (see Section 16). The corrected finding is Alfred William’s own Masonic affiliation, separate from (not a continuation of) his father Alfred Edmund Knott’s Cherwell Lodge No. 599 affiliation in England (Section 11.2) — father and son, two distinct lodges, two countries.
